Offensive security research · static + dynamic reverse engineering

TopFollow v8.4.5-Beta
complete security teardown

A full reverse-engineering of com.nivaroid.topfollow — an Instagram follower-exchange manipulation app that advertises itself as “very secure” with anti-tamper, anti-Frida, SSL pinning and Play Integrity. Every claimed protection was located, decoded and defeated. The Instagram login flow, follow-task verification, coin economy and the OLLVM-obfuscated native library are reconstructed end to end.

73Vulnerabilities
11Critical
25High

12Low
8Frida PoCs
26Backend endpoints
22Native JNI fns
4,146Classes decompiled
Headline findings. The app stores the user's real Instagram password and TOTP 2FA seed in an unencrypted Room database, protected only by a keyless byte-transform cipher that was re-implemented and round-tripped in Python. It harvests Instagram session cookies from a WebView and synthesises an OAuth bearer. It sends the live Instagram session token to its own backend in a plaintext Token header on every request. Its coin reward is switched on by a client-asserted get_coin=true flag, with a client-supplied order_value and a static, replayable challenge token. And because allowBackup=true ships with completely empty exclusion rules, all of it is copied into the user's Google cloud backup.

1. Executive summary

TopFollow is a follower-exchange service: users surrender an Instagram credential, the app drives that account to follow, like, comment on, save and view other users' content in exchange for coins, and coins are spent to buy engagement for the user's own account. The vendor markets strong protection — an obfuscated native library, certificate pinning, signature self-verification, anti-Frida scanning, root detection and Play Integrity.

All of it was defeated statically before a single process was instrumented. The native library libtopfollow.so is OLLVM control-flow-flattened across all 22 JNI functions, but flattening hides control flow, not data. Resolving the 943 position-independent call $+5; pop reg; add reg,K thunks per function recovered 760 .rodata references, and an exhaustive single-byte XOR sweep over keys 1–127 cracked every secret with just two keys: 0x55 and 0x5A. The backend URL, all Instagram endpoints, the certificate pin, the anti-Frida keyword list and the complete root-path list were recovered from the binary.

The Java layer is worse. The “encryption” protecting Instagram passwords is a four-stage keyless byte transform — XOR 0x6C, rotate-left 3, reverse, XOR (i*37)^0xA5 — which was re-implemented in Python and verified to round-trip. The Room database has no SQLCipher and no passphrase. The manifest sets allowBackup="true" and then ships two empty backup-rule resources, so the default include-everything behaviour applies and the credential store is copied to Google's cloud.

The business logic is the most serious problem. Coin rewards are gated on get_coin, a boolean the client sets to "true" whenever its in-memory copy of the Instagram response says status == "ok". The reward amount order_value also comes from the client. The anti-replay token x4 is a compile-time constant, TFjMTZRk5rMHdTVR, identical on every install forever. The purchase price is computed client-side from a server-pushed rate multiplied by a client-chosen count, and checked against a wallet stored in the local unencrypted database — if the balance is too low the app simply sets the Buy button's click listener to null.

One design decision dominates everything else. Because the app accepts the Instagram password rather than using OAuth, and because it forwards the live session token to top.nivafollower.app in a Token header, the vendor operator holds mass account-takeover capability over the entire user base by construction — no exploit required. Any compromise of that server, or of the ServerCheck channel that supplies the base URL and certificate pin at runtime, yields every account at once.

Severity distribution

Critical · 11 High · 25 Medium · 25 Low · 12

The five findings that matter most

  1. CRED-01 / CRED-02 — passwords and TOTP seeds at rest. instagram_accounts.u_w and two_factors.u_p/s_k in an unencrypted SQLite file behind a keyless cipher.
  2. BIZ-01 / BIZ-02 — client-declared rewards. get_coin and order_value both come from the client on order/syncOrder.php.
  3. TRAN-02 / CRYP-04 — remotely rotatable base URL and pin. ha.h builds its Retrofit from SharedPreferences Pin/PinActive, written by the ServerCheck bootstrap.
  4. CRED-04 — session-token exfiltration by design. d.t() puts the Instagram token in a plaintext header on every backend call.
  5. STOR-02 — empty backup exclusions. <full-backup-content/> and <data-extraction-rules><cloud-backup/></data-extraction-rules> with allowBackup=true.

Signing certificate — recovered without apksigner or a JDK

The APK has no v1/JAR signature (no META-INF/*.RSA|*.SF|*.MF). The certificate was extracted by parsing the APK Signature Scheme v2 pair directly out of the signing block: magic APK Sig Block 42 at 0x9d1608, v2 pair at 0x9ce620 (id=0x7109871a, u64 length 1514), central directory at 0x9d1618, certificate DER 864 bytes.

Serial
1  (first certificate ever generated with this key)
Validity
2023-12-15 17:44:33 UTC → 2048-12-08  (25 years, no rotation path)
Key / algo

Cert SHA-256
d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e
SPKI pin
sha256/4xnmMuV3jXL9RYz2ZVYtWWrkkv/6wSuz8SkHAHAV5Uo=
The certificate digest is the tamper-check constant. The pin literal found in libtopfollow.so .rodata — sha256/d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e — is byte-for-byte the SHA-256 of this APK's own signing certificate. One constant is doing two unrelated jobs: it is both the “certificate pin” and the value the signature self-check compares against. That is not transport pinning (it says nothing about the server's TLS key) and it is not a strong integrity anchor (it is compared in hookable Java reflection). See CRYP-04 and INTE-01.

3. Threat model

Assets

AssetWhere it livesWho wants it
Instagram passwordinstagram_accounts.u_w, two_factors.u_p — unencrypted SQLite, keyless cipherVendor operator, local attacker, anyone with the Google backup
Instagram TOTP seedtwo_factors.s_kSame — permanently defeats 2FA
Instagram session / bearerinstagram_accounts.u_a, .token, WebView cookie jarVendor operator (receives it in a header), MITM
Coin / gem balancedevice.coin, device.gem (local) + server ledgerUsers wanting free engagement
Backend base URL + pinSharedPreferences Pin/PinActive, from ServerCheckMITM wanting to redirect the whole fleet
Device identityAid, DeviceId, RD, fcm_token, hash_key, nonceTrackers; correlates accounts across installs
Instagram platform integrity—Attacked by the product itself: fake engagement at scale

Actors and capability

ActorCapabilityPrimary routeFindings
Vendor operatorFull server control, sees every requestNo exploit needed — the app sends Instagram passwords (instagramLogin.php) and live session tokens (Token header) by designCRED-04, CRED-05
Malicious user (rooted own device)Frida, Magisk, sqlite3, file accessForce get_coin=true, inflate the wallet, replay the static x4BIZ-01, BIZ-02, BIZ-03, BIZ-06
Network attacker (same Wi-Fi, rogue AP, hostile CDN)TLS MITMHook CertificatePinner.check, or simply wait for ServerCheck to hand over a new pinTRAN-01, TRAN-02, CRYP-04
Local attacker (stolen phone, forensic tool, other profile)Read app data / backupPull the unencrypted Room DB or the Google cloud backup; decrypt with the keyless cipherSTOR-01, STOR-02, CRYP-01
Google-account attackerCloud restoreBackup exclusion rules are empty, so the credential DB restores onto their deviceSTOR-02
RepackagerModify + re-sign the APKHook MessageDigest.digest(), or patch the embedded constantINTE-01, CRYP-02, INTE-12
Instagram (defender)Abuse detection, bansEvery user presents an identical hard-coded device fingerprint, bloks id and app versionCRED-06, CRED-07, CRED-08
The end user (victim)NoneSurrenders a password or session, is never re-asked for consent, and gets automated on indefinitelyCRED-01, STOR-15, BIZ-08

Trust boundaries

User→ TopFollow client→ BOUNDARY 1 — credential surrender→ top.nivafollower.app
TopFollow client→ BOUNDARY 2 — private-API impersonation→ Instagram
ServerCheck response→ BOUNDARY 3 — URL + pin injection→ Retrofit / CertificatePinner

Boundary 3 is the structural flaw: the app takes its transport trust anchor from the network it is supposed to be protecting against. An attacker who controls one ServerCheck response controls boundaries 1 and 2 as well, because the redirect carries the Instagram password with it.

Attack trees

Free engagement (money)
1. Spawn with 01 + 02 so the app boots clean.
2. Attach 06: force InstagramResponse.getStatus() → "ok".
3. ha.c.onReady() now emits get_coin="true" for tasks that failed or never ran.
4. Optionally rewrite order_value to choose the payout.
5. x4 is a constant, so nothing blocks replay.
→ coins accumulate without performing Instagram actions; spend them via 05.
Fleet-wide credential capture (network)
1. MITM the device; attach 02 to defeat the OkHttp pinner (it is Java-side — the .so has no TLS code).
2. Or, cleaner: hijack the ServerCheck response and set url + pin to attacker values; the app writes them to Pin/PinActive and rebuilds its Retrofit through native q.k().
3. Every subsequent call — including instagramLogin.php — goes to the attacker with the pin already trusting the attacker's certificate.
→ Instagram passwords and session tokens for the whole user base.
Offline credential theft (no root)
1. allowBackup=true and both backup-rule resources are empty.
2. Cloud backup / device-to-device migration copies t_f_d_b_f_v_c and TOPFVC_Shared.xml.
3. Open the SQLite file; apply the four-stage keyless transform in reverse.
→ passwords, bearers and TOTP seeds, with no device access at all.
Repackaging (bypass anti-tamper)
1. Modify the APK, re-sign with any key.
2. Spawn with 01: MessageDigest.digest() returns the expected d845591e…, d8.f.a() returns true, maps/root/Frida probes are filtered, System.exit is suppressed.
3. Or statically patch the embedded constant (CRYP-02).
→ a trojanised build that passes every self-check.

What is genuinely well built

  • Small exported surface. Only three components are exported — the launcher activity and two framework receivers, both permission-guarded. No deep links, no exported services or providers, no exported TaskActionReceiver (STOR-16).
  • Sound binary hardening flags. libtopfollow.so has FULL RELRO + BIND_NOW, a stack canary and FORTIFY on all three ABIs.
  • Real obfuscation effort. OLLVM control-flow flattening across all 22 JNI functions, scrambled DEX map_list, single-byte XOR string tables, nested base64 literals and a JNI-only export surface. This meaningfully raises the cost of casual analysis — it simply does not raise the security floor (INTE-06, INTE-07).
  • Correct transport defaults. usesCleartextTraffic="false".
  • Server-issued order stamps exist. order_stamp/order_stamp2 are real server nonces — the design was reaching for replay protection, it just left x4 static and get_coin client-side.

4. Architecture & data flow

The DEX is aggressively name-obfuscated and many real classes are hidden inside unrelated package names — the cipher lives in com.bumptech.glide.d, the ServerCheck bootstrap in com.bumptech.glide.manager.r, the backend request dispatcher in androidx.fragment.app.e, and the Play Integrity gate in d3.d. The map below is the de-obfuscated picture.

ComponentReal role
com.bumptech.glide.dCipher + header factory. o() base64-decode, p() decrypt, q() encrypt, b() URL-encode, t(InstagramAccount) backend headers.
com.bumptech.glide.manager.rServerCheck bootstrap. Fetches ServerCheckModel and writes Pin/PinActive.
com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.qThe 22-method JNI facade into libtopfollow.so. Every secret-producing function is native.
com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.a0Challenge constants. x() → the 64-hex integrity constant; x4() → the static claim token; x0..x3() read Sign/RID/RD/SND.
ha.h / ha.kBackend Retrofit (built by native q.k()) and its single generic POST method a(path, headers, body).
ha.b, ha.c, ha.a, ha.d, ha.iRequest-body builders for login/submitOrder, syncOrder claim, captcha/getMainInfo, miner, image.
androidx.fragment.app.eEndpoint dispatcher. A switch over 18 cases mapping to account/*.php, plus the coin-cost purchase dialog.
d3.dPlay Integrity gate (l()): checks SND/RID, caches the token 6 h, injects x2.
d8.fGoogle Phonesky verification (a(Signature[])) — must pass before attestation.
ia.q / ia.g / ja.eInstagram layer. ia.q builds the three Retrofits and the header map; ia.g is the private-API interface; ja.e is the per-account task worker.
ia.v / ia.s / ia.xLogin. ia.v orchestrates the Bloks/CAA login and stores credentials; ia.s parses the response by substring match; ia.x is the human-behaviour simulator.
oa.l1 / WebViewActivityWebView cookie harvester — the second login path.
DoTasksServiceFarming engine. One ja.e worker per active Instagram account.
CaptchaRequesthCaptcha / reCAPTCHA chooser; site keys come from native q.f()/q.g().
MyDatabase (c2.x)Unencrypted Room DB t_f_d_b_f_v_c.

End-to-end request path

UI / DoTasksService→ body builder (ha.*)→ d3.d.l() integrity inject→ native q.t/q.i/q.u/q.v→ d.t() headers→ ha.k.a(path, headers, body)→ OkHttp + CertificatePinner
ja.e task worker→ ia.x human sim→ native q.h(Order) signed_body→ ia.q.f() IG headers→ ia.g.i()/d0()→ Instagram

5. Instagram login flow

There is no OAuth. The app reimplements Instagram's confidential Android login (the “Bloks / CAA” flow) and offers a second path that simply steals session cookies from a WebView.

Path A — private-API Bloks login (ia.v, ia.s)

1. ia.q headers→ 2. POST com.bloks.www.caa.login.login_homepage→ 3. ia.s.success() substring-parse→ 4a. Bearer → ia.v.a() stores creds
4b. two_step_verification → ia.i0 challenge→ 4c. challenge_required / errors → onLogin("fail"/"show_error")

The login body is URL-encoded form data: params={base64(client_input_params + server_params)}&bk_client_context={base64(bloks_version, styles_id)}&bloks_versioning_id=083f38c3…39d604.

client_input_params

  • username_input — the user's Instagram handle
  • aac — JSON string {"aac_init_timestamp":..,"aacjid":"..","aaccs":".."}
  • lois_settings.lois_token = ""
  • cloud_trust_token = null, zero_balance_state = "", network_bssid = null

server_params

  • device_id = android-{Aid}, qe_device_id
  • login_surface = login_home, login_entry_point = logged_out
  • waterfall_id, family_device_id
  • offline_experiment_group = caa_iteration_v3_perf_ig_4
  • access_flow_version = pre_mt_behavior
  • INTERNAL__latency_qpl_instance_id = 19558568600088
  • INTERNAL__latency_qpl_marker_id = 36707139
  • is_from_logged_out/is_platform_login/is_from_logged_in_switcher = 0
  • layered_homepage_experiment_group = Deploy:+Not+in+Experiment

Response handling — substring matching, not JSON parsing

ia.s.success() strips backslashes and then string-searches the raw body. Order of checks:

Substring foundOutcome
"Bearer"→ ia.v.a(): split on logged_in_user/headers/Bearer/hmac, build the account row, store the password
"two_step_verification"→ extract challenge_context by indexOf("(dkc"), set instance_id via regex (\d+)\s+"com\.bloks\. (default 4806706160933748992), marker_id=36707139, launch ia.i0
"The password you entered is incorrect"→ onLogin("show_error","password_incorrect")
"We can't find an account with" / "Please check your username and try again."→ onLogin("show_error","can_not_find_account")
"there was a problem with your request"→ onLogin("show_error","Sorry, there was a problem with your request.")
"challenge_required"→ onLogin("fail","challenge_required")
none of the above→ generic error + " code:13"

What gets persisted on success (ia.v.a())

setU_w(com.bumptech.glide.d.q(v6.getPassword())) — the Instagram password, run through the keyless cipher.
setU_a(com.bumptech.glide.d.q("Bearer " + token)) — the OAuth bearer.
Plus pk, username, mid, rur="CLN", claim (from the hmac fragment, else "0"), family_device_id, pigeon_session_id, profile_pic_url, and active/sort. The new row id is written to SharedPreferences ActiveID. Retry logic: up to 3 attempts (ia.v.b()) before onLogin("fail","connection").

Path B — WebView cookie theft (oa.l1.onPageFinished)

Every time a page finishes loading in WebViewActivity, the client reads the Instagram cookie jar:

String cookie = CookieManager.getInstance().getCookie("https://www.instagram.com/");
String sessionid  = y(cookie, "sessionid");
String ds_user_id = y(cookie, "ds_user_id");
String mid        = y(cookie, "mid");

if (sessionid && ds_user_id) {
    JSONObject o = new JSONObject();
    o.put("ds_user_id", ds_user_id);
    o.put("sessionid",  sessionid);
    String b64 = Base64.encodeToString(o.toString().getBytes(UTF_8), NO_WRAP);
    String bearer = "Bearer IGT:2:" + b64;          // <-- synthesised OAuth token

    account.setPk(ds_user_id);
    account.setU_a(glide.d.q(bearer));              // stored, keyless-ciphered
    account.setUsername("empty");
    account.setU_w("");                             // no password on this path
    account.setAccount_type("auth");
    account.setMid(mid); account.setRur("CLN"); account.setClaim("0");
    account.setActive(1);
    // inserted into instagram_accounts; ActiveID updated
}

No user gesture, no confirmation dialog, no domain restriction beyond “a page finished loading”. The account is then usable for every automation the app performs. This is CRED-03.

Two-factor handling

The two_factors table stores u_n (username), u_p (password) and s_k (the shared secret). TwoFactorLoginActivity drives ia.a with a c2.q callback; c2.t loads rows with the raw query select * from two_factors. The backend endpoints account/getQuestions.php, account/getSecretKey.php and account/requestDigitCode.php participate, and ha.c sends an s_k_2fa field. Storing the seed means 2FA is not a second factor at all — see CRED-02.

6. Instagram private API layer

Three Retrofit instances, all built in native code

Factory callBase URLUsed for
helper.q.l(0)https://b.i.instagram.com/api/v1/Private mobile API — follow, like, comment, save
helper.q.l(1)https://i.instagram.com/api/v2/Private API v2
helper.q.l(2)https://www.instagram.com/graphql/query (+ https://www.instagram.com/)GraphQL / web-path actions

All three are constructed by libtopfollow.so!x0018d3f7 — the same function that performs the signature self-check and the anti-Frida scan. The URLs are stored as triple-base64 in .rodata.

Automation reach beyond follower exchange

The DEX references direct_v2/has_interop_upgraded/ (Instagram ↔ Facebook DM interop), create_note/v2/ (Threads notes), seen/ (story views), /save/, three Facebook doc_id GraphQL documents, attestation/create_android_keystore/, and the token prefix 3,IGbdd5f76a8fb9c4f86adf36a09f2750dc,{pk}. Per-account rows also store fbid_v2 and interop_messaging_user_fbid, linking the Instagram identity to its Facebook counterpart. See STOR-14.

signed_body

Every action request passes com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.q.h(Order) — native x0011f1a2 — as Instagram's signed_body. Because the signing key lives in the binary and the input is the locally held Order object, a hook on q.h() yields valid signatures for arbitrary orders (script 03 logs each one; script 06 rewrites the order first).

7. Follow-task verification logic

DoTasksService is the farming engine. On onCreate() it enumerates every instagram_accounts row where isActive() is true and builds one ja.e worker per account. If SharedPreferences SingleTasking is false, all workers run in parallel; otherwise only the first starts. Each worker reads its task list from MyDatabase.setup().n().getActionList() and filters on SharedPreferences NewTaskType (default all).

Task dispatch — ja.e.b() switches on Order.getType()

typeActionCallRequest body (all carry signed_body = q.h(Order))
0FOLLOW<code>ia.g.i(q.h(order), url, form)</code><code>include_follow_friction_check=1, user_id=&lt;target pk&gt;, radio_type=wifi-none, _uid, device_id=android-{aid}, _uuid, nav_chain, container_module=profile</code>
1LIKE<code>ia.g.i(q.h(order), url, form)</code><code>delivery_class=organic, tap_source=button, media_id, radio_type=wifi-none, _uid, _uuid, nav_chain, is_carousel_bumped_post=false, container_module=feed_short_url, feed_position=0</code> + <code>&amp;d=0</code>
3REPOST / Threads note<code>ia.g.i(q.h(order), url, form)</code><code>media_client_position, media_id, note_style=13, text=, _uuid, nav_chain, audience=7, event_source=ufi, container_module=feed_contextual_profile</code>
4SEEN (story views)<code>ia.q.g.create(...).m(...)</code>JSON body ending in <code>force_seen_story_ids: []</code>
5COMMENT<code>ia.g.d0(headers, form)</code>headers gain <code>x-ig-nav-chain=...,GVw:comments_v2_feed_timeline:5:button:</code>, <code>x-ig-salt-ids=220140399,974460658</code>; form <code>media_id, comment_session_id=&lt;random UUID&gt;, comment_text</code>
6SAVE<code>/save/</code><code>media_id</code> + signed body

Verification: what the server actually receives

After the action returns, ha.c.onReady(JsonObject) assembles the claim for order/syncOrder.php. Every field is client-produced:

FieldSourceNote
x4order.getOrder_stamp2(), else "empty"Server-issued order stamp (second).
x5helper.q.a(gson.toJson(instagramResponse))Native transform of the raw IG response — the only “proof” of the action.
x6order.getOrder_stamp(), else "empty"Server-issued order stamp.
x7instagramResponse.getMessage()Raw IG message string.
typeorder.getOrder_type()Task category string.
i_typeorder.getType() (int)0=follow, 1=like, 3=repost, 4=seen, 5=comment, 6=save.
order_idorder.getOrder_id()Server order id.
order_valueorder.getOrder_value()Client-supplied reward amount.
pk / usernamethe target accountWho was followed / liked.
get_coin(response.getStatus().equals("ok")) ? "true" : "false"Client-asserted success flag — the money switch.
account_type(acct.getAccount_type()=="web") ? "1" : "0"Cookie-harvested web account vs private-API account.
active_pkcurrentInstagram().getPk()The acting (hijacked) account.
get_new_orderthis.p (boolean)Ask the server for another task in the same round-trip.
new_order_typethis.qWhich task type to receive next.
is_single_taskingSharedPreferences SingleTaskingClient-chosen concurrency mode.
The verification is the vulnerability. The literal code is: 
if ((v3_15 == null) || ((v3_15.getStatus() == null) || (!v3_15.getStatus().equals("ok")))) {
    v1_16 = "false";
} else {
    v1_16 = "true";
}
p9.addProperty("get_coin", v1_16);
v3_15 is the in-memory InstagramResponse. Forcing getStatus() to return "ok" — one Frida overload — makes the client assert payment for an action that failed or never occurred. The reward amount (order_value) is also read from the local Order, and the anti-replay token x4 is the compile-time constant TFjMTZRk5rMHdTVR. Nothing in this payload requires the server to have issued anything fresh for this specific attempt. See BIZ-01, BIZ-02, BIZ-06, BIZ-07.

Server-issued stamps — present but insufficient

order_stamp, order_stamp2 and order_stamp3 are server-issued nonces carried on the Order model, and sign is a per-order signature. The design clearly intended replay protection. It fails because (a) the stamps authenticate the order, not the completion; (b) the completion evidence x5 is a client-side native transform of a client-held response object; and (c) the money switch get_coin sits outside any signed material.

8. Coin economy

Earn — complete tasks for other users

ja.e performs IG action→ha.c builds claim→POST order/syncOrder.php→server credits coins

Spend — buy engagement for your own account

UI: pick count→cost = coin_per_X * count→if device.coin ≥ cost: enable Buy→set_order_stamp = q.s(user,count,type)→POST order/submitOrder.php

Rates are server-pushed but consumed client-side

getMainInfo.php populates the Room app_info row. The purchase dialog then computes the price locally — MyDatabase.setup().n().getCoin_per_like() * selectedCount (and the equivalents for follow, comment, repost, save, seen, threads) — and compares it against MyDatabase.setup().getDevice().getCoin(), a row in the local unencrypted database. The affordability decision is expressed purely as UI state:

int cost = MyDatabase.setup().n().getCoin_per_like() * count;
if (MyDatabase.setup().getDevice().getCoin() < cost) {
    priceView.setTextColor(getColor(R.color.red));
    dialog.findViewById(R.id.buyWithCoins).setOnClickListener(null);   // disabled
} else {
    priceView.setTextColor(getColor(R.color.green));
    dialog.findViewById(R.id.buyWithCoins)
          .setOnClickListener(new oa.i0(activity, count, dialog, 0));  // enabled
}
// identical block for gems, via getDevice().getGem()

Hooking DeviceModel.getCoin()/getGem() to return a large number, or editing the device row directly with sqlite3, makes every purchase button live. See BIZ-03 and BIZ-05.

Wallet schema

ColumnMeaning
device.coinCoin balance — the spendable currency for followers/likes/comments
device.gemPremium currency, spent through the parallel Buy-with-Gems button
device.tokenBackend Top-Token header value
instagram_accounts.collected_coinsPer-account lifetime earnings
instagram_accounts.is_vipLocal mirror of VIP tier
app_info.coin_per_*Server-pushed earn/spend rates per action type
app_info.min_*_orderMinimum order sizes

Secondary earn paths

  • Coupons — account/addCoupon.php, getCoupons.php (Coupon, CouponList, CouponType models).
  • Gift codes — account/getGiftCodeReward.php.
  • Daily rewards — account/checkDailyGift.php, getDailyItems.php; eligibility is evaluated against the device clock.
  • Referrals — account/getInviteData.php, setInviteCode.php; identity travels as client-set by / user_pk.
  • Leaderboard & miners — getLeaderBoard.php, getMinerRequests.php, changeMinerRequest.php.
  • VIP — account/upgradeAccountToVip.php with vip_stamp = q.m(), gated only on locally available media_count and username.
No client-side throttling anywhere on the money path. The only pacing value, app_info.action_delay, governs Instagram action timing — not claim rate. request_id is a locally generated UUID.randomUUID(), so deduplication depends entirely on server enforcement that the client gives no evidence of. See STOR-10 and BIZ-10/11.

Base URL construction

Generic POST surface

ha.k declares a single method — a(String path, HashMap headers, RequestBody body) — so the whole API is reached through one dynamically-parameterised call. That is why endpoint discovery required decoding the base64 literals rather than reading Retrofit annotations (which the scrambled DEX map_list makes unreadable anyway).

Play Integrity integration (d3.d.l())

  • Gate: SP.getBoolean("SND",false) == true && SP.getInt("RID",0) == 3850153 — both client-writable (INTE-08).
  • Precondition: d8.f.a(signatures) must confirm the Play Store package certificate (INTE-02).
  • Nonce: RD + helper.q.j() + "877665803231" — native timestamp plus a constant GCP project number, no server randomness (INTE-10).
  • Caching: result stored in RIT (timestamp), AIT, RD; reused while younger than 6 hours (INTE-09).
  • Transmission: body field x2 = d.q(d.p(SP"RD")).
  • Fallback: SafetyNet stubs are present but inert.

How the flattened functions were defeated

Static CFG tracing through an OLLVM state dispatcher is impractical, but the data references are not flattened. Each function obtains its .rodata base through a position-independent thunk of the form:

call  $+5            ; push address of the next instruction
pop   reg            ; reg = current PC
add   reg, K         ; reg = PC + K  -> the function's private GOT/.rodata base
...
mov   rax, [reg + off]   ; load a string pointer relative to that base

Detecting all 943 such thunks and computing a per-function base recovered 760 .rodata references — enough to attribute every secret string to the function that uses it. The bootstrap function x0018d3f7 alone resolves 622 references, which is how the signature-verification path, ANDROID_ID, DeviceModel setters, the three base URLs, the anti-tamper keyword list and certificatePinner were all tied to a single entry point.

Bootstrap mega-function x0018d3f7 — attributed responsibilities

  • Builds all three Instagram Retrofit instances (q.l(0|1|2)) with the triple-base64 URLs.
  • Verifies the APK signature: getPackageInfo(signatures) → reflective MessageDigest("SHA-256") → compare with d845591e….
  • Scans /proc/self/maps for Xposed/LSPosed/EdXposed/Riru/Zygisk/Substrate/libbridge.so, plus (deleted) libart/libc mappings and rwxp regions.
  • Tests base64-encoded Frida markers and probes nine su paths.
  • Reads ANDROID_ID, Build.DEVICE, Build.HARDWARE; populates DeviceModel (setHash_key, setNonce, setHash_type, addDevice).
  • Calls CertificatePinner$Builder.add(...) and wires the OkHttp client.
Correction to a first-pass conclusion. An earlier reading of this library reported “no crypto in the .so”. That was wrong, and the cause matters: the reference resolver discarded any .rodata target that was not ≥92% printable, so the AES S-box, inverse S-box and rcon — high-entropy binary tables — were filtered out of every listing. Keeping binary references (work/aes_forensics.py, work/aes_xref.py) and then executing the code under Unicorn (work/unicorn_aes.py) established that the genuine AES tables are present and used in all three ABIs. A later functional test then showed the cipher is AES-shaped but not proven standard AES-256 — see the correction in section 11.6.
What is still true. The protection logic — signature verification, maps scanning, root probing, pin installation — is executed by calling back into Java, because the library has no TLS and no filesystem-scanning primitives of its own. That places the entire protection surface on the wrong side of the JNI boundary, where Frida operates natively. Script 01 defeats all of it without modifying a single byte of the .so. The AES is used internally by the request pipeline and is not reachable from Java: none of the 22 JNI signatures is ([B)[B (CRYP-09).

11. Native AES — discovery, and execution proof under Unicorn

This section exists because a first-pass conclusion was wrong

The initial analysis of libtopfollow.so reported “no crypto and no TLS code in the library”. That was incorrect, and the failure mode is worth recording precisely because it is easy to repeat.

The reference resolver used in the first pass kept a .rodata target only if it decoded to a string that was at least 92 % printable. The AES S-box, inverse S-box and rcon are high-entropy binary tables, not strings — so every reference to them was silently discarded, and the resulting listings made the library look crypto-free. Two fixes recovered the truth: keep binary references (work/aes_forensics.py, work/aes_xref.py), and then execute the code rather than infer from it (work/unicorn_aes.py).

A second bug compounded it. A first attempt to derive the AES S-box from first principles computed the multiplicative inverse as pow(i, 254, 0x11b). That is invalid — GF(28) is not ℤ/0x11b, so modular exponentiation returns the wrong element. A second attempt rotated a running accumulator instead of the original inverse. Both produced a plausible-looking but wrong table, and both made the scanner report “no AES S-box present” for a binary that contains one. The shipped script derives the inverse by search under gmul (validated against the FIPS-197 worked examples 0x57×0x83=0xc1 and 0x57×0x13=0xfe), cross-checks the result against a transcribed literal, and aborts rather than reporting a false negative if the two disagree.

11.1 The tables, located by content in all three ABIs

Each table was found by exact byte-pattern search, then verified two ways: the forward S-box was re-derived from first principles in GF(28), and the inverse table was confirmed to be the exact permutation inverse of the forward one (inv[sbox[i]] == i for all 256 i, and sorted(table) == range(256)).

inverse S-box0x139b00xec800x817052 09 6a d5 30 36 a5 38CRYP-09
rcon (14 bytes)0x13b100xede00x82d001 02 04 08 10 20 40 80CRYP-10

11.3 Per-section entropy — the packing hypothesis tested and rejected

Entropy is the standard test for packing: a packed or compressed .text scores above 7.8. This library does not.

SectionAddressSizeEntropyReading
.text0x2d2ac1,591,5686.8621Normal compiled ARM64. A packed section scores >7.8. Not packed.
.rodata0x118b037,5476.8779Held up almost entirely by the three AES tables; the rest is ordinary literals.
.gcc_except_table0x1ab5c26,3125.6261Landing pads.
.eh_frame0x2344040,5564.78671,090 well-formed FDEs — the source of every function boundary used here.
.rela.dyn0x10e065,4242.8076Relocations.
.data.rel.ro0x1b616017,5520.1154vtables / pointers.
whole file—1,805,4006.7518No region above 7.0 except the S-boxes themselves.
The two highest-entropy 4 KiB windows in the entire file are 0x12000–0x13000 at 7.9954 and 0x13000–0x14000 at 7.9613. Those are precisely the AES S-box and inverse S-box — a 256-byte table of all 256 byte values has near-maximum entropy by construction. Everything else in the file peaks at 6.93. There is no region anywhere that could hold a megabyte-scale white-box table: .rodata is 37,547 bytes and the whole file is 1,805,400.

11.5 Unicorn execution — the proof

The DSO was mapped at VA 0 (its first PT_LOAD has vaddr 0, so VA equals file offset), with stack, heap and TLS regions, and every GOT slot repointed at a unique trampoline so that a UC_HOOK_CODE hook could service each libc call from Python. Without that, the PLT stubs branch through a zeroed GOT and the CPU jumps to PC=0 — which is exactly how the first attempt failed.

=== UNICORN AArch64 AES HARNESS ===
  target: work/apk/lib/arm64-v8a/libtopfollow.so
  FIPS-197 AES-256 reference schedule computed: 240 bytes (15 round keys)
    RK0 = 603deb1015ca71be2b73aef0857d7781
    RK1 = 1f352c073b6108d72d9810a30914dff4
    RK14= fe4890d1e6188d0b046df344706c631e

  image 1,806,336 B mapped at VA 0

11.6 Reading the result — and correcting an over-claim

What the key-expansion run genuinely proves

  • The routine returns normally after 89,148 instructions — it is not dead code and it does not need a live JNI environment to run its crypto path.
  • It reads the genuine AES forward S-box 112 times and rcon exactly 14 times. Fourteen rcon reads is the AES-256 count (AES-128 uses 10, AES-192 uses 12), and it validates w3 == w4 == 0x20 = a 256-bit length.
  • The S-box reads have a clean period-14 structure. An instruction-level histogram (work/unicorn_round_structure.py → out/42) shows the 112 reads come from exactly 8 distinct instructions, each hit exactly 14 times — two clusters of four (0x3283c/40/58/88 and 0x33840/44/48/c8). Four S-box lookups × 14 rounds is precisely AES-256 SubWord inside the key schedule. This is the single strongest structural signal that the expansion is AES-256.
  • It writes a 240-byte schedule = 15 × 16 bytes, exactly the AES-256 round-key size.
  • So the library contains and uses the real AES S-box, inverse S-box and rcon, and the key expansion is structurally AES-256. That much is solid.

Correction: the block ciphers are not validated — and neither extreme claim is warranted

An earlier revision called the whole thing “proven AES-256” and said the schedule “obeys the FIPS-197 recurrence exactly”. A functional test (work/unicorn_verify_cipher.py, work/unicorn_roundtrip.py) forced a correction — but the honest position is inconclusive, not the opposite extreme. What the tests actually show:

  • The captured 240-byte output is not a standard schedule. Checked word-by-word it breaks the strict FIPS-197 recurrence at w[8] (4c3ce189 produced vs e539061e required). But this output sits at ctx+0x30, while the routine's own schedule pointers ctx+0x438/0x458 were left pointing at zeroed buffers — so ctx+0x30 may be scratch, and the keyexp almost certainly never ran against a faithful ctx either.
  • The encrypt/decrypt blocks were never run faithfully. On a zeroed schedule the encrypt block 0x2fdcc returns early after 23,186 instructions having read the S-box only 32 times (4 instructions × 8) — a truncated path, not a 14-round SubBytes (~160 reads). When the schedule buffers are populated, the same function does not terminate: it hits the 3,000,000-instruction cap with 0 S-box reads, because the OLLVM control-flow-flattened dispatcher never reaches an exit state without the exact C++ object layout. Neither run is the real cipher.
  • The round-trip “failure” is therefore not evidence of a non-AES cipher. decrypt(encrypt(pt)) ≠ pt only because both calls took truncated early-exit paths on a zeroed schedule — it does not show the functions are non-inverse in a faithful run.

Honest conclusion. The AES tables are real, genuinely referenced and genuinely read; the key expansion is structurally AES-256 (period-14 SubWord over the true S-box, 14 rcon reads, 240-byte output). The block ciphers could not be validated under emulation because faithfully driving them needs the precise ctx object layout (key string, IV, mode, GCM/CTR counter) that a standalone Unicorn call does not reproduce — the flattened dispatcher either early-exits or spins. So the correct claim is “an AES-table cipher with an AES-256-shaped key expansion”, not “proven standard AES-256” and not “proven not-AES”. 09_native_aes_dump.js settles it on a device by capturing the schedule and I/O the running app actually uses and testing them against a reference AES.

Still not recovered — the key

  • The run was given the FIPS-197 AES-256 test key in a caller buffer laid out three ways (libc++ short-string, long-string, {ptr,len,cap} triple). All three runs were byte-identical — same instruction count, same table reads, same schedule. The expansion does not use the caller's key; a read trace (11.9) shows the caller key buffer is never even read.
  • Brute force over every 16-, 24- and 32-byte window of the 1,805,400-byte file (5,416,128 candidates) found no window expanding to the observed first round key 1742e227063cdfce2c2b4cbd71f1297a.
  • Which JNI function reaches it. All 22 natives are named and mapped (11.10) and none has a ([B)[B signature, so the cipher is not exposed to Java as a byte-array primitive. Which of the 22 calls it internally is not resolved statically (the x86 PIC resolver cannot attribute the table refs; arm64 materialises its table at runtime) — script 09 settles it live.

11.7 The observed 240-byte output

Read straight out of emulated memory at ctx+0x30 after the key-expansion run. It is AES-256-sized (240 bytes) but, as 11.6 records, the bytes at ctx+0x30 do not satisfy the strict FIPS-197 recurrence and may be a scratch buffer rather than the schedule the routine actually uses (its ctx+0x438/0x458 schedule pointers stayed zeroed). Read it as “round-key-shaped output of an incompletely-initialised run”, not as a standard AES key schedule.

11.8 A dead JNI bridge

At arm64 0x106660 and 0x162c4c the library calls JNIEnv slot 33 (ldr x8,[x8,#0x108], 0x108 / 8 = 33) = GetStaticMethodID, asking class com/nivaroid/topfollow/helper/T for a method named digest with descriptor ([B)[B. Enumerating the DEX directly, helper.T declares exactly two methods — o(Ljava/lang/String;)Ljava/lang/String; and sd(Ljava/lang/String;)Ljava/lang/String;. There is no digest, and no ([B)[B method exists in any application class. The lookup returns NULL and raises NoSuchMethodError. See CRED-11.

0x106644  ldr  x8, [x19]            ; JNIEnv*
0x106648  ldr  x8, [x8, #0x108]     ; slot 33 = GetStaticMethodID
0x10664c  ldr  x1, [sp, #0x38]      ; clazz  = helper/T
0x106650  adrp x2, #0x14000
0x106654  adrp x3, #0x14000
0x106658  mov  x0, x19
0x10665c  add  x2, x2, #0xb88       ; -> "digest"
0x106660  add  x3, x3, #0xcc4       ; -> "([B)[B"
0x106664  blr  x8                   ; returns NULL -> NoSuchMethodError

.rodata cluster around the lookup:
  0x14b2f  "F3AES"                                   <- referenced by nothing (CRYP-14)
  0x14b35  "(Lretrofit2/Response;)Ljava/lang/String;"
  0x14b5e  "(ZLjava/lang/String;)Lretrofit2/Retrofit;"

11.9 Where the AES key comes from — a full read trace

Because the routine produced an identical schedule under three different caller-key layouts, the key is clearly not the caller's. To find its real source, work/unicorn_key_trace.py re-runs 0x32158 with a per-instruction memory-read logger and classifies all 9,173 reads by region.

RegionReadsBytesWhat it is
stack6,37727,616OLLVM flattening scratch + the state dispatcher's spill slots
image:data1,9008,384File-backed constants. 1,288 of these are the single byte at 0x0 (the ELF 0x7f magic, read by the flattened dispatcher). The rest are 52 scattered single-byte reads of a high-entropy table at 0x13b30 — see below.
heap7702,984The ctx struct (0x810020c–0x81003d4) read byte-by-byte, plus the 16-byte plaintext at IN. The caller-supplied key buffer is never read.
SBOX112112Forward S-box — AES-256 SubWord during expansion
RCON1414Round constants — 14 = AES-256

A fourth AES-related table that the first pass missed

Immediately after rcon (0x13b10 + 14 = 0x13b1e) sits a 549-byte high-entropy table at 0x13b30 (entropy 7.8099, 244/256 distinct bytes). The key expansion reads 52 scattered single bytes from it. It is not the S-box, not the inverse, not a permutation of 0..255, and not a GF(28) log/antilog table — it is a key-derivation / whitening table. The earlier content-scan (aes_forensics.py) looked only for the five canonical AES patterns and so missed it entirely; it surfaced only by tracing what the code actually reads.

  === VERDICT ===
  RK0 is NOT present in any file-backed region read by 0x32158,
  nor anywhere in the 1.8 MB file. The key is COMPUTED at runtime
  from register/immediate state inside the OLLVM prologue, not read
  from a stored constant. Static extraction is impossible; it is
  recoverable only from live process memory (dynamic-lab/09).
Verdict, and it is now evidence-backed rather than inferred. The observed round key RK0 = 1742e227063cdfce2c2b4cbd71f1297a appears in no file-backed region the routine reads, and in no byte window of the 1,805,400-byte file (5,416,128 candidates brute-forced). The key is therefore computed at runtime — the whitening table at 0x13b30 and the 16-byte constant at 0x13b1e are folded together with the ctx state inside the OLLVM-flattened prologue to produce the cipher's runtime key. Static extraction is not possible; the key is recoverable only from live process memory, which is exactly what 09_native_aes_dump.js does (CRYP-10).

11.10 JNI attribution — the 22 natives, independently verified

The report attributes all 22 native functions to their Java wrappers in section 10. That mapping was re-derived here from a completely independent direction and confirmed exactly. On the x86 build the JNINativeMethod table is stored as literal virtual-address pointers at file offset 0xd8fec (imagebase 0, so name/signature pointers index straight into .rodata and function pointers into .text). Parsing all 22 triples gives the registered name, JNI signature and entry offset for each; every name was then confirmed to be a declared private static native method of com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.q in the DEX, and every one maps to a public wrapper q.a()…q.v().

Confirmed from the table itself: none of the 22 signatures is ([B)[B. The natives exchange String, JsonObject, Order, InstagramAccount, retrofit2.Response and retrofit2.Retrofit — never a raw byte array. So the AES proven above is used inside these request-building and response-parsing routines and is not exposed to Java as a byte-array cipher (CRYP-09). Artefacts: work/out/35_x86_jni_full.json, work/out/39_jni_full_map.json.

What remains genuinely open is not which Java method reaches the AES (all 22 are named and mapped) but which of them calls it internally: on arm64 the AES sits under 22 call-graph ancestors of root 0xfe268, and the x86 PIC-thunk resolver could not be made to attribute the table references because ebx is clobbered between the thunk and the access. 09_native_aes_dump.js settles this on a device by Stalker-following whichever JNI native actually touches the S-box at runtime.

12. Cryptography & obfuscation

The keyless cipher — com.bumptech.glide.d.p() / .q()

Both directions run the same four stages; decryption is the exact inverse. There is no key, no IV, no per-install entropy and no server involvement.

// ENCRYPT (glide.d.q) - stored form is base64( native q.o( core(plaintext) ) )
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] ^= 0x6C;                              // 1. fixed XOR
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] = ((buf[i] & 7) << 5) | (buf[i] >> 3); // 2. rotate-left 3
for (lo=0, hi=len-1; lo<hi; lo++, hi--) swap(buf[lo], buf[hi]);           // 3. reverse
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] ^= ((i * 37) ^ 0xA5) & 0xFF;           // 4. positional XOR

// DECRYPT (glide.d.p) - input is base64-decoded native q.n( stored )
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] ^= ((i * 37) ^ 0xA5) & 0xFF;           // undo 4
for (lo=0, hi=len-1; lo<hi; lo++, hi--) swap(buf[lo], buf[hi]);           // undo 3
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] = ((buf[i] & 0x1F) << 3) | (buf[i] >> 5); // undo 2 (ror 3)
for (i = 0; i < len; i++)  buf[i] ^= 0x6C;                                // undo 1

This was re-implemented in Python and verified to round-trip on passwords, bearer tokens, TOTP seeds and URLs. What it protects: instagram_accounts.u_w (Instagram password), .u_a (bearer), two_factors.u_p and .s_k, and the SharedPreferences values Sign, Aid, DeviceId, RD, Pin, plus the x2 integrity field and the xr message field.

13. Data storage

Room database t_f_d_b_f_v_c — unencrypted

MyDatabase.setup() builds it through a plain Room builder. There is no SQLCipher dependency, no SupportFactory passphrase and no Jetpack Security usage anywhere in the DEX.

Backup configuration — the empty-rule finding

The manifest sets android:allowBackup="true" and points both rule attributes at resources. Both were extracted from resources.arsc and decoded from binary AXML:

@7F160000 → res/Qq.xml (132 bytes)

<full-backup-content/>

@7F160001 → res/4j.xml (212 bytes)

<data-extraction-rules>
  <cloud-backup/>
</data-extraction-rules>
Neither file contains a single <exclude> element. An empty <full-backup-content/> means “back up everything”, and <cloud-backup/> with no children means the same for device-to-device transfer. So the unencrypted t_f_d_b_f_v_c database — Instagram passwords, TOTP seeds, session tokens, the coin wallet — and the whole of TOPFVC_Shared are copied into the user's Google account and into any new device during migration. An attacker who compromises the Google account, or who briefly holds the phone during a transfer, obtains every credential without ever touching the app's runtime protections. See STOR-02.

Other manifest observations

AttributeValueAssessment
allowBackuptrueCritical with empty rules
fullBackupContent / dataExtractionRulesset but emptyNo exclusions
usesCleartextTrafficfalseGood
networkSecurityConfigabsentMissing declarative pinning
extractNativeLibsfalse.so stays inside the APK, trivially extractable
debuggable / testOnlynot setGood
Exported components3 (launcher + 2 guarded framework receivers)Small surface
Requested permissionsINTERNET, FOREGROUND_SERVICE(_SPECIAL_USE), POST_NOTIFICATIONS, ACCESS_NETWORK_STATE, WAKE_LOCK, c2dm.RECEIVEReasonable — no storage/contacts/location

14. Anti-tamper & integrity controls — and how each one falls

The app advertises five protections. All five were located precisely and all five are defeated by script 01_anti_tamper_killer.js without patching the binary.

ControlImplementationBypassFinding
Signature self-checkx0018d3f7 → getPackageInfo(GET_SIGNATURES) → reflective MessageDigest("SHA-256") → compare d845591e…Hook MessageDigest.digest() and Signature.toByteArray(); return the expected digestINTE-01
Play Store cert gated8.f.a(Signature[]) compares base64url SHA-256 against Google's Phonesky pinsOne-line hook returning trueINTE-02
Anti-Frida / anti-Xposed/proc/self/maps keyword scan (XOR 0x5A) + base64 Frida markers + (deleted) and rwxp heuristics + TCP 27042/27043 probesSanitise read()/fgets() output line-by-line; neuter strstr()/strcmp(); refuse the port connectsINTE-03
Root detectionNine hard-coded su paths via open/access/stat and File.exists(); Runtime.exec("su")Force ENOENT on the probes; return false from exists(); throw on execINTE-04
Emulator detectionBuild.DEVICE, Build.HARDWAREOverwrite the Build statics — the app already hard-codes SM-E625F/exynos9825 for Instagram, so the spoof is consistentINTE-05
Play IntegrityGated on SP SND==true && RID==3850153; token cached 6 h; nonce RD+q.j()+877665803231; sent as x2Hook SharedPreferencesImpl.getBoolean/getInt to skip or fake the gate entirelyINTE-08, INTE-09, INTE-10
Native obfuscationOLLVM CFF on all 22 functions; XOR 0x55/0x5A strings; nested base64; scrambled DEX map_list; JNI-only exportsResolve the 943 PIC thunks and read the data references; brute-force 127 XOR keys; intercept RegisterNativesINTE-06, INTE-07, CRYP-07
Kill switchesSystem.exit, Process.killProcess, Runtime.exitAll three suppressedINTE-01
The common thread. libtopfollow.so contains no TLS code and imports no crypto library (its own AES-table cipher is used only inside the request pipeline and is not reachable from Java as a byte-array primitive — CRYP-09), so every “native” protection is executed by calling back into the Java framework. That puts the whole integrity story on the side of the JNI boundary where instrumentation frameworks already live. The obfuscation is competent and it did cost real effort — but it delays analysis rather than preventing tampering, and none of it changes what the server is willing to trust.

16. Detailed findings

73 findings · each with mechanism, impact, evidence, proof-of-concept script and remediation.

BIZ-01
Critical Business logic

Coin reward flag get_coin is asserted by the client

How it works

When a task finishes, ha.c.onReady(JsonObject) sets get_coin to "true" if and only if the in-memory Instagram response object reports getStatus().equals("ok"), then POSTs the claim to order/syncOrder.php. The status is a client-side object; forcing it to "ok" makes the client claim payment for an action that never happened or that Instagram rejected.

Impact

Unbounded coin generation. Coins are the currency spent on followers and likes, so this converts directly into free engagement for any account the attacker names — the core fraud of the entire service.

Evidence

ha.c.java:78 p9.addProperty("get_coin", v1_16) with v1_16 = (resp.getStatus()==null || !resp.getStatus().equals("ok")) ? "false" : "true"

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Determine the reward exclusively server-side, from an independently fetched Instagram state plus a server-issued task nonce. Never accept a client-declared success flag.

BIZ-02
Critical Business logic

Reward amount order_value travels from the client

How it works

The same claim body carries order_value = order.getOrder_value(), taken from the local Order object, and type = order.getOrder_type(). The client therefore states how much the completed task is worth. A hook on Order.getOrder_value() rewrites the amount before the request is even built.

Impact

Direct monetary inflation: the attacker chooses the payout. Even if the server re-derives the amount, the field's presence shows the client is trusted with pricing on the money path.

Evidence

ha.c.java:69 p9.addProperty("order_value", v4_8), :62 addProperty("type", v1_11.getOrder_type()); models.Order.getOrder_value()

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Look up the reward from the server's own copy of the order keyed by order_id; ignore any client-supplied value.

CRED-01
Critical Credentials

Instagram account PASSWORD persisted on-device in a reversible cipher

How it works

The private-API login path stores the victim's real Instagram password in the Room table instagram_accounts, column u_w. The value passed to setU_w() is com.bumptech.glide.d.q(password) — a keyless static byte transform, not encryption. Any process or backup that can read the app's data directory recovers the password in one line.

Impact

Full Instagram account takeover. The password (not just a session) is captured, so an attacker can also reset 2FA, change the e-mail and permanently lock the victim out. Every account the user ever adds is exposed.

Evidence

ia.v.a(ia.v,String) → v7_1.setU_w(com.bumptech.glide.d.q(v6.getPassword())); Room DDL instagram_accounts(... `u_w` TEXT ...)

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Never accept or store the user's Instagram password. Use Instagram's official OAuth (which this app does not); at absolute minimum store only a short-lived session token in Android Keystore-backed EncryptedSharedPreferences.

CRED-02
Critical Credentials

2FA seed / backup codes stored in the two_factors table

How it works

A second Room table two_factors holds u_n (username), u_p (password) and s_k (shared secret). c2.t issues the raw query select * from two_factors and populates TwoFactorAccount.setU_p(). s_k is the TOTP seed used by account/getSecretKey.php / requestDigitCode.php, and ha.c sends an s_k_2fa field to the backend.

Impact

Defeats two-factor authentication entirely: with the seed an attacker generates valid 6-digit codes forever. Password (CRED-01) plus this seed is a complete, permanent compromise.

Evidence

c2.t:627 S("select * from two_factors"), :660 setU_p(...); models.TwoFactorAccount.getS_k()/getU_p(); addProperty("s_k_2fa", ...)

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Never store a TOTP seed. If 2FA recovery is genuinely required, wrap it in an AndroidKeyStore AES-GCM key bound to user authentication with hardware attestation.

CRED-03
Critical Credentials

WebView cookie harvester synthesises an OAuth bearer token from sessionid

How it works

oa.l1.onPageFinished() reads CookieManager.getCookie("https://www.instagram.com/"), extracts sessionid, ds_user_id and mid, then builds "Bearer IGT:2:" + Base64({"ds_user_id":..,"sessionid":..}) and writes it to instagram_accounts.u_a. This is silent session-token theft performed on any page load, with no user gesture or confirmation.

Impact

Account takeover without ever learning the password. The synthesised bearer is exactly what Instagram's own mobile clients use, so it is fully functional for follow / like / comment / DM automation.

Evidence

oa.l1.java — y(cookie,"sessionid"), y(cookie,"ds_user_id"), "Bearer IGT:2:" + Base64.encodeToString(...), v13_3.setU_a(com.bumptech.glide.d.q(v8_9))

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Do not harvest cookies from a WebView. Use official Instagram OAuth with PKCE and store only the returned, scoped, expiring access token.

CRED-04
Critical Credentials

Instagram session token sent to the vendor backend in a plaintext HTTP header

How it works

com.bumptech.glide.d.t(InstagramAccount) builds the header map attached to every backend call and puts the victim's Instagram session token straight into Token: account.getToken(), together with Active-Id: account.getPk() and Top-Token: device.getToken().

Impact

The operator of top.nivafollower.app receives live Instagram session credentials for every user on every request — a server-side mass account-takeover capability requiring no client compromise at all.

Evidence

com.bumptech.glide.d.t(): v0_1.put("Token", p4.getToken()), put("Active-Id", p4.getPk()), put("Top-Token", ...getDevice().getToken())

Proof of concept

03_instagram_api_intercept.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Session tokens must never leave the device. Perform Instagram actions locally and send the backend only opaque, non-replayable result attestations.

CRED-05
Critical Credentials

Instagram login credentials forwarded to instagramLogin.php

How it works

ha.b.onReady() case 0 calls native helper.q.r(json, q8.t1.k(), DeviceId) (libtopfollow.so!x0015b1e9) to build the body of a POST to the base64-obfuscated endpoint aW5zdGFncmFtTG9naW4ucGhw = instagramLogin.php. The username and password entered for Instagram are therefore also transmitted to the third-party TopFollow server.

Impact

Vendor-side credential phishing by design. Combined with CRED-01 the operator holds both the live session and the long-term password for every user of the app.

Evidence

ha.b.java:30 a(com.bumptech.glide.d.o("aW5zdGFncmFtTG9naW4ucGhw"), d.t(0), ...); endpoint decoded from the DEX literal

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Remove the endpoint. Instagram authentication belongs between the user and Instagram only.

CRYP-01
Critical Cryptography

Keyless static cipher used for all “encrypted” local secrets

How it works

com.bumptech.glide.d.p() / .q() apply a fixed four-stage transform with no key material whatsoever: XOR every byte with 0x6C; rotate each byte left by 3; reverse the whole buffer; XOR byte i with (i*37) ^ 0xA5. It was re-implemented in Python and round-trips perfectly. This single routine protects the Instagram password, the bearer token, the 2FA seed and every sensitive SharedPreferences value.

Impact

Obfuscation marketed as encryption. Anyone who pulls the APK — or merely a backup — decrypts every secret offline in microseconds, with no key-recovery step at all.

Evidence

com.bumptech.glide.d.p()/q(); verified by report/cipher_poc.py (round-trip OK for passwords, bearers, TOTP seeds and URLs)

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Use androidx.security.crypto.EncryptedSharedPreferences / EncryptedFile with an AndroidKeyStore master key, or SQLCipher for Room.

STOR-01
Critical Data storage

Room database is unencrypted

How it works

MyDatabase.setup() builds the database named t_f_d_b_f_v_c through a plain Room builder (c2.u(context)). There is no SQLCipher, no SupportFactory passphrase and no Jetpack Security usage anywhere in the DEX. The file contains Instagram passwords (u_w), bearer tokens (u_a), TOTP seeds (s_k), session tokens, the coin/gem wallet and the FCM token.

Impact

Any local attacker — rooted device, malicious app with backup access, forensic tool or stolen phone — reads the full credential and wallet store with sqlite3. The only protection over the values is the keyless cipher of CRYP-01.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.db.MyDatabase.setup(); Room DDL strings for device, instagram_accounts, two_factors, app_info

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Encrypt with SQLCipher under an AndroidKeyStore-resident key, and stop storing passwords and TOTP seeds at all (CRED-01, CRED-02).

STOR-02
Critical Data storage

allowBackup=true with completely empty backup-exclusion rules

How it works

The manifest declares android:allowBackup="true" with android:fullBackupContent="@7F160000" and android:dataExtractionRules="@7F160001". Both resources were decoded from the ARSC and are empty shells: <full-backup-content/> (132 bytes) and <data-extraction-rules><cloud-backup/></data-extraction-rules> (212 bytes). Neither contains a single <exclude> element, so the default include-everything behaviour applies to the database and shared_prefs directories.

Impact

The unencrypted Room database — Instagram passwords, TOTP seeds, session tokens — plus all of TOPFVC_Shared, is copied into the user's Google cloud backup and into any device-to-device transfer. An attacker who compromises the Google account, or who borrows the phone during a migration, obtains every credential without ever touching the device.

Evidence

res/Qq.xml = <full-backup-content/>; res/4j.xml = <data-extraction-rules><cloud-backup/></data-extraction-rules>; out/21_backup_rules.txt

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js

Remediation

Set allowBackup="false", or add explicit <exclude domain="database" path="t_f_d_b_f_v_c"/> and <exclude domain="sharedpref" path="TOPFVC_Shared.xml"/> to both rule files.

TRAN-02
Critical Transport

Backend base URL and pin are downloaded at runtime

How it works

ha.h constructs its Retrofit with helper.q.k(com.bumptech.glide.d.p(SP.getString("Pin","")), SP.getBoolean("PinActive",false)). The stored Pin blob and PinActive flag are written by the ServerCheck bootstrap from the network response. The current decoded value is https://top.nivafollower.app/v840/.

Impact

A single compromised or intercepted ServerCheck response redirects the whole application — including instagramLogin.php, which carries the user's Instagram password — to an attacker-controlled host, with the attacker's certificate pinned as trusted.

Evidence

ha.h.java q.k(d.p(Pin), PinActive); com.bumptech.glide.manager.r stores Pin/PinActive; decoded URL from libtopfollow.so XOR 0x55

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js 02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js

Remediation

Hard-code the production base URL and pin in the binary. If rotation is required, sign the ServerCheck payload with a build-time embedded public key and verify it natively.

BIZ-03
High Business logic

Client-side price computation for buying engagement

How it works

The purchase dialog computes cost = MyDatabase.setup().n().getCoin_per_like() * selectedCount (and the equivalent for follow/comment/repost/save/seen/threads) and compares it against MyDatabase.setup().getDevice().getCoin() — a row in the local Room device table. If the balance is insufficient the Buy button's click listener is simply set to null; otherwise new oa.i0(activity, count, dialog, 0) is attached.

Impact

The affordability decision never reaches the server before the button becomes clickable. Inflating the local device.coin value, or hooking DeviceModel.getCoin(), enables every purchase path in the UI.

Evidence

androidx.fragment.app.e (default branch) and ha.a.onReady(): int cost = n().getCoin_per_like() * count; if (getDevice().getCoin() < cost) ... setOnClickListener(0)

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Enforce price and balance server-side on order/submitOrder.php, and treat the client figure as display-only.

BIZ-04
High Business logic

set_order_stamp signature omits balance, currency and nonce

How it works

Before submitting an order the app calls native helper.q.s(username, order_count, type) (libtopfollow.so!x0017b62c) and attaches the result as set_order_stamp. The signed input is exactly three fields: target username, requested count and action type. The coin/gem balance, which currency is spent, any server nonce and the requesting account are all outside the signature.

Impact

The stamp cannot prove the buyer could pay. A replayed or edited request that preserves those three fields carries a valid stamp while the payment context around it is entirely attacker-chosen.

Evidence

ha.b.java:34 p11.addProperty("set_order_stamp", q.s(json.get("username"), json.get("order_count"), json.get("type")))

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Sign the complete canonical request body including account id, server nonce, timestamp and price, and verify server-side.

BIZ-05
High Data storage

Local wallet in an unencrypted Room database is the balance of record for the UI

How it works

The Room database t_f_d_b_f_v_c is opened with a plain SupportSQLiteOpenHelper — there is no SQLCipher dependency and no SupportFactory passphrase anywhere in the DEX. Table device holds coin and gem; instagram_accounts holds 42 columns of credentials; two_factors holds passwords and TOTP seeds; app_info holds the server-pushed economy rates.

Impact

On any rooted device, or from a cloud backup (STOR-02), the entire wallet and credential store is readable and writable with the sqlite3 CLI. Balances can be edited at rest, not merely hooked at runtime.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.db.MyDatabase.setup() (no passphrase / SupportFactory); Room DDL for device, instagram_accounts, two_factors, app_info

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js 05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Encrypt the database with SQLCipher under an AndroidKeyStore key, and keep the authoritative balance server-side so a local edit has no effect.

BIZ-06
High Business logic

Static challenge token x4 accepted on the claim endpoint

How it works

order/syncOrder.php receives x4 from helper/a0.x4(), which is the compile-time constant TFjMTZRk5rMHdTVR (see CRYP-03). It carries no device, session, order or time binding.

Impact

The one field meant to make a claim non-replayable is identical for every user, every device, forever, so any captured claim can be replayed — or fabricated from scratch.

Evidence

ha.c.java:39 p9.addProperty("x4", v3_2) from a0.x4()

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Replace with a per-task server nonce bound to the order id and the device attestation.

BIZ-07
High Business logic

Claim body built entirely on-device by native code with no server challenge

How it works

helper.q.t(JsonObject, InstagramAccount, Order) (x0015a3b7) together with q.i/q.u/q.v assemble the whole syncOrder.php payload locally, including x5 = q.a(gson.toJson(instagramResponse)), x6 = order_stamp, x7 = response.getMessage(), active_pk, get_new_order, new_order_type and is_single_tasking (read from SharedPreferences). None of these values is issued or countersigned by the server for that specific attempt.

Impact

A scripted client can construct a syntactically perfect, internally consistent claim without ever talking to Instagram, because every input to the payload is available locally or forgeable.

Evidence

ha.c.java:24-99; natives x0015a3b7, x0014b4f3, x00120b1e, x00135e2a, x0012e5a1

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js 07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

BIZ-08
High Instagram API

Mass multi-account farming worker with a client-controlled concurrency flag

How it works

DoTasksService enumerates every row of instagram_accounts where isActive() is true and spawns one ja.e worker per account. If SharedPreferences SingleTasking is false, all workers run in parallel rather than only the first.

Impact

The app is architecturally an engagement-farming bot: one device drives N hijacked Instagram accounts simultaneously. Flipping a single boolean multiplies the abuse rate, and it is entirely under the user's control.

Evidence

DoTasksService.onCreate()/a(); SP.getBoolean("SingleTasking", true); ja.e(InstagramAccount, Context, d3.d) per active account

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js 06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

This is the product's core abuse; there is no fix short of not automating other people's accounts.

BIZ-12
High Integrity

Server-pushed update and download links are opened without verification

How it works

ServerCheckModel carries update_available, update_url and repair_mode, and app_info carries download_link, shop_link, support_link and channel_link. The base URL and pin come from the same channel. There is no signature or digest check over the update payload.

Impact

Whoever can MITM or compromise the ServerCheck response can point every install at an arbitrary APK or web page. Because the pin also arrives on that channel (CRYP-04), the MITM can pin itself first and then serve the update.

Evidence

models.ServerCheckModel{url,pin,pin_active,repair_mode,update_available,update_url}; app_info(download_link, shop_link, support_link, channel_link); bootstrap in com.bumptech.glide.manager.r

Proof of concept

02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Distribute updates only through Play's in-app update API, and sign any out-of-band payload with a key embedded at build time.

CRED-06
High Instagram API

Hard-coded spoofed Instagram client identity

How it works

The app impersonates one specific real device/app build: User-Agent Instagram 369.0.0.46.101 Android (33/13;420dpi;1080x2269;samsung;SM-E625F;f62;exynos9825;en_US;785863906), x-ig-app-id: 567067343352427, x-bloks-version-id: 083f38c334f42c5e3322bb77464c601e8882cd9ff2d30ac915ba7a497539d604, x-ig-capabilities: 3brTv10=.

Impact

Instagram can trivially fingerprint and mass-ban every TopFollow user, since all of them present the same device model, app version and bloks id. It is also a ToS violation that endangers the user's account.

CRED-07
High Instagram API

Per-request forged x-ig-nav-chain / x-ig-salt-ids / x-fb-rmd

How it works

Every action request fabricates a navigation chain that makes an automated call look like organic UI navigation, e.g. ShortUrlFeedFragment:feed_short_url:1:warm_start:{ts-5}.{ts+8}::, ContextualFeedFragment:feed_contextual:11:button:..., GVw:comments_v2_feed_timeline:5:button:, plus four sets of salt ids such as 220145826 and 220140399,974460658. Timestamps are derived from System.currentTimeMillis() and split at index 10.

Impact

Deliberate anti-fraud evasion against Instagram's abuse detection. The automation is engineered to be indistinguishable from a human — exactly what platform-integrity systems exist to catch.

CRED-08
High Instagram API

Human-behaviour simulator with jittered timings and forced re-login

How it works

ia.x performs a warm-up burst before every real action — typeahead lookup, profile view, media tab, inbox, quick-promotions — with timestamps offset −2 s to −12 s and 100–999 ms random jitter. If last_login is older than 1800 s it re-authenticates first, so the session pattern mimics a real user returning to the app.

Purpose-built defeat of behavioural bot detection. Raises the blast radius for Instagram (fake engagement at scale) and for the user (account suspension).
