CRYP-02
High Cryptography

Hard-coded 64-hex integrity constant reconstructed from nested base64

How it works

helper/a0.x() concatenates the literal ZDgzZmI3Y2U3ZjlhNzM5MjJkMjI2MmNlM with d.o("MlEzWXpoak56aGxOR014TWpFeFpHRXhOMlkwTldJNU9UQmhNelE0WkRRd05XSTFPRFkzTnc9PQ==") and base64-decodes the result, yielding d83fb7ce7f9a73922d2262ce3d7c8c78e4c1211da17f45b990a348d405b58677. It matches none of SHA-256(APK), SHA-256(classes.dex), SHA-256(libtopfollow.so), SHA-256(package name) or SHA-256(backend URL) — it is a bare hard-coded comparison constant.

Impact

A static embedded comparison value is a patch-one-byte bypass: replace the constant with the digest of your own modified build, or hook the comparator. Layering base64 over it adds no security, only friction for analysts.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.a0.x(); decoded chain reproduced in the analysis log

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Use Play Integrity API verdicts evaluated server-side. Client-side digest comparison cannot be made tamper-proof.

CRYP-03
High Business logic

Static, fully replayable challenge blob x4 in every coin claim

How it works

helper/a0.x4() builds "VkVacVRW" + d.o("VW1GVmJYTXhZMnN4U1ZwRw==") + "UldVZz09" and double-base64-decodes it to the constant TFjMTZRk5rMHdTVR. No device id, timestamp, server nonce or counter enters the computation, so the value is identical on every install forever.

Impact

Whatever anti-replay purpose x4 was meant to serve is void: the same token can be replayed by any client indefinitely. Security theatre embedded directly in the money path.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.a0.x4() → TFjMTZRk5rMHdTVR; consumed by ha.c.onReady() as the x4 field of order/syncOrder.php

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Issue a single-use server nonce per task, bind it to the order id and the device attestation, and expire it server-side.

CRYP-04
High Transport

Certificate pin is the APK signing-certificate digest, and it is server-rotatable

How it works

The pin literal recovered from libtopfollow.so .rodata is sha256/d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e. That is byte-for-byte the SHA-256 of the APK's own signing certificate (self-signed, CN=Maryam Ahmadi, OU=Android Developer, O=NivaRoid, L=Shiraz, ST=Fars, C=IR, serial 1, RSA-2048, valid 2023-12-15 → 2048-12-08). The same digest is also the tamper-check value, so one constant does two unrelated jobs. Worse, the live pin is whatever the server pushed into SharedPreferences Pin / PinActive.

Impact

Pinning a signing certificate rather than a TLS leaf/intermediate SPKI is meaningless for transport security — it pins nothing about the server's key. And because the pin arrives from the network, a MITM or a compromised ServerCheck response can install an attacker pin and then intercept everything.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so .rodata (XOR 0x55) pin literal; APK Signature Scheme v2 block at file offset 0x9ce620 (cert DER 864 bytes); ha.h → q.k(d.p(Pin), PinActive)

Proof of concept

02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Pin the SPKI of the real TLS certificate plus a backup, ship the pins inside the binary, and never accept a pin from a network response.

CRYP-05
High Native hardening

No TLS stack and no crypto imports in the native library — pinning is Java-side only

How it works

libtopfollow.so imports no crypto and no TLS symbols (DT_NEEDED = libz/libandroid/liblog/libm/libdl/libc); checksec shows FULL RELRO + BIND_NOW, stack canary and FORTIFY, but the only exported symbol is JNI_OnLoad. All pinning, certificate handling and Retrofit construction happen through JNI calls back into Java (OkHttp CertificatePinner). The library does implement its own AES-256 internally (CRYP-09), but that is used inside the request pipeline and is not what protects the TLS channel.

Impact

The entire “native hardening” story collapses at the Java boundary: hooking okhttp3.CertificatePinner.check in Frida defeats the pinning without touching a single instruction of the .so. The obfuscation buys time, not security.

Evidence

out/13_so_checksec.json; import table contains no SSL_*, no EVP_*, no *crypt*; sole export JNI_OnLoad

Proof of concept

02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js

Remediation

If pinning must resist instrumentation it needs a native TLS stack (e.g. statically linked BoringSSL) with verification performed entirely in native code.

CRYP-09
High Cryptography

Native AES-table cipher with an AES-256-shaped key expansion; block ciphers not validated under emulation, and unreachable from Java

How it works

libtopfollow.so contains a compact, table-based cipher built on the real AES tables in all three ABIs. The canonical FIPS-197 forward S-box, inverse S-box and rcon were located by byte-pattern search — arm64-v8a 0x128b0/0x139b0/0x13b10, x86_64 0x0db80/0x0ec80/0x0ede0, x86 0x07070/0x08170/0x082d0 — and each was verified to be the true AES table by independent derivation in GF(28) and by confirming that the inverse table is the exact permutation inverse of the forward table. Five functions reference them: 0x2dc00, 0x2fdcc (S-box, encrypt), 0x2eb94, 0x30f18 (inverse S-box, decrypt) and 0x32158 (S-box and rcon, key expansion), behind wrappers 0x34424, 0x35518, 0x38fa4, 0x3a838. Running 0x32158 under Unicorn AArch64 with libc trampolines executed 89,148 instructions, returned normally, read rcon exactly 14 times (the AES-256 count), validated w3/w4 against #0x20=32, and wrote a 240-byte (15×16) output. An instruction-level histogram (work/unicorn_round_structure.py, out/42) shows its 112 S-box reads come from exactly 8 distinct instructions each hit 14 times — the period-14 SubWord structure of AES-256 key expansion. The key expansion is therefore structurally AES-256. The block ciphers, however, could not be validated under emulation: on a zeroed schedule the encrypt block 0x2fdcc returns early after 23,186 instructions with only 32 S-box reads (4 instructions × 8, a truncated path, not a 14-round ~160-read SubBytes), and with populated schedule buffers it does not terminate (3,000,000-instruction cap, 0 S-box reads) because the OLLVM control-flow-flattened dispatcher never reaches an exit state without the exact C++ ctx object layout (key string, IV, mode, GCM/CTR counter). The apparent decrypt(encrypt(pt))≠pt is a consequence of both calls taking truncated paths, not proof of a non-AES cipher. So the correct claim is “an AES-table cipher with an AES-256-shaped key expansion”, not “proven standard AES-256” and not “proven not-AES”. Separately, none of the 22 RegisterNatives methods has a ([B)[B signature, and no com.nivaroid.topfollow method anywhere in the DEX has that descriptor, so whatever this cipher is, it is not directly callable from Java as a byte-array primitive.

Impact

A table-based block cipher built on the real AES S-box is compiled into the shipping binary and used internally by the native request/signature pipeline, but it is invisible to any Java-level audit, to the app's own developers reviewing the Java layer, and to any tester who only instruments Java. Crypto that cannot be reached by the documented interface cannot be reviewed, rotated, disabled or tested — it is an unauditable primitive sitting in the trust path of every signed request. That even its exact algorithm resists validation without the live object layout makes it less reviewable still: an analyst cannot safely assume textbook AES semantics. It also means an analyst who only examines the DEX will conclude, wrongly, that the app performs no native encryption at all.

Evidence

S-box/inverse/rcon located and verified in all three ABIs (work/out/30_aes_forensics_arm64.txt); five referencing functions and the 22-ancestor call graph (work/out/32_aes_xref_arm64-v8a.json); Unicorn key-expansion log, 112 S-box + 14 rcon reads (work/out/33_unicorn_aes_arm64.txt); period-14 S-box-read IP histogram for keyexp and the 4×8 encrypt pattern (work/out/42_round_structure.txt); block-cipher runs that early-exit / spin, showing they are unvalidated (work/out/40_verify_cipher.txt, work/out/41_roundtrip.txt); 22 JNI signatures from the DEX, none ([B)[B (work/out/39_jni_full_map.json)

Proof of concept

09_native_aes_dump.js

Remediation

Do not ship cryptographic primitives that no documented interface reaches, and do not ship a cipher whose exact algorithm cannot be validated without the live object layout. Either expose it through an auditable, standards-conformant API with a rotatable key, or remove it. Publish the key-management story for whatever remains.

CRYP-10
High Cryptography

Native AES key is runtime-derived: it is not the caller's key and not a byte window of the file

How it works

The Unicorn run of the key-expansion routine 0x32158 was given a FIPS-197 AES-256 test key in a caller buffer laid out three different ways (libc++ short-string, libc++ long-string, and a plain {ptr,len,cap} triple). All three runs were byte-identical: 89,148 instructions, 112 S-box reads, 14 rcon reads, and the same resulting schedule. The expanded schedule therefore does not depend on the key supplied by the caller. A full per-instruction read trace (work/unicorn_key_trace.py, 9,173 reads) shows the caller key buffer is never read at all; instead the routine reads the ctx struct byte-by-byte and pulls 52 scattered single bytes from a 549-byte high-entropy table at .rodata 0x13b30 (entropy 7.8099, immediately after rcon, preceded by a 16-byte constant 9a2f5ebc…c591 at 0x13b1e). That table is not the S-box, not the inverse, not a permutation and not a GF(28) log table — it is a key-derivation / whitening table that the first content-scan missed because it only looked for the five canonical AES patterns. Brute-force over every 16-, 24- and 32-byte window of the 1,805,400-byte file (5.4 million candidates) found no window that expands to the observed RK0 1742e227063cdfce2c2b4cbd71f1297a, and RK0 appears in no file-backed region the routine reads. The key is computed inside the OLLVM-flattened prologue at runtime by folding the 0x13b30 table, the 0x13b1e constant and the ctx state together.

Impact

The cipher's key cannot be extracted by reading the binary, and cannot be influenced by the caller — so whatever this AES protects is keyed by a value that exists only during execution. That makes the protection opaque even to the vendor's own Java-side code review, and it means the key is either a hardcoded derivation (recoverable by one dynamic trace, and then identical on every install forever) or depends on device state (in which case it is not portable and silently breaks recovery and migration). Neither is a sound key-management design.

Evidence

work/out/34_aes_key_recovery.txt: observed RK0..RK14; 5,416,128 file windows scanned, no match; three caller-buffer layouts producing identical schedules. work/out/37_key_source_trace.txt + 38_key_source_summary.txt: the 9,173-read trace, reads-by-region, and the 0x13b30 whitening-table hexdump. work/unicorn_key_trace.py reproduces it.

Proof of concept

09_native_aes_dump.js

Remediation

Derive keys with a documented KDF from a server-issued secret and a device-bound AndroidKeyStore key. Never key a production cipher from an opaque in-binary computation.

CRYP-12
High Cryptography

The RSA public key that seals the password is supplied by the network at runtime and never validated

How it works

ia.m.java:215-218 reads two Instagram response headers — Ig-Set-Password-Encryption-Key-Id and Ig-Set-Password-Encryption-Pub-Key — into InstagramReqInfo.key_id and .pub_key. ia.v.java:259 then passes them straight into q8.t1.f(), which base64-decodes the key, strips -(.*)-|\n, wraps it in an X509EncodedKeySpec and uses it to seal the user's Instagram password. There is no allow-list of expected keys, no key-length or algorithm check, no certificate validation and no comparison against any previously seen key. Scanning all three ABIs for RSA SubjectPublicKeyInfo DER prefixes (30820122300d06092a864886f70d010101), modulus prefixes and PEM headers confirms no RSA public key is embedded in the binary — the trust anchor for the password is entirely network-supplied.

Impact

Anyone who can present a response to the app — a MITM who has defeated the Java-side pinning, a hostile network, or an attacker who has hijacked the runtime-supplied ServerCheck pin (see TRAN-02 and CRYP-04) — can install their own RSA public key. The app will then seal the victim's real Instagram password to the attacker's key, hand over the complete #PWD_INSTAGRAM:4: blob, and the attacker decrypts it offline. The password is captured in plaintext without ever breaking the encryption. This composes directly with the ServerCheck pin-injection flaw: one hijacked bootstrap response yields the entire user base's Instagram passwords.

Evidence

ia.m.java:215-218 header capture; com.nivaroid.topfollow.models.InstagramReqInfo fields key_id, pub_key; q8.t1.java:52-58 decode + X509EncodedKeySpec with no validation; absence of any RSA DER/PEM in all three ABIs

Proof of concept

10_java_crypto_layer.js 02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Validate the supplied public key: enforce a minimum modulus size, require RSA, and compare its fingerprint against a pinned allow-list or a signed key-distribution channel. Reject the login rather than sealing to an unexpected key.

INTE-01
High Integrity

APK signature self-check is hookable at the Java reflection layer

How it works

The bootstrap mega-function x0018d3f7 (registered as helper.q.l(int)) calls PackageManager.getPackageInfo(pkg, GET_SIGNATURES), then reflectively obtains MessageDigest.getInstance("SHA-256") and compares the digest against the embedded constant. Because the whole check is expressed as ordinary JNI calls into java.security.MessageDigest and android.content.pm.Signature, hooking digest() or Signature.toByteArray() returns whatever value the check expects.

Impact

The primary anti-repackaging control is defeated with roughly ten lines of Frida, allowing a modified, re-signed APK to run while believing itself genuine.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so!x0018d3f7 references (per out/18_native_strings_by_fn.txt): getPackageInfo, signatures, SHA-256, MessageDigest, certificatePinner, ANDROID_ID, DeviceModel

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Rely on the Play Integrity API verdict, verified server-side against Google's keys. Client self-inspection is always defeatable.

INTE-02
High Integrity

Google Play Store certificate gate d8.f.a() forced open

How it works

d8.f.a(Signature[]) (“PhoneskyVerificationUtils”) SHA-256/base64url-compares the Play Store package's signatures against 8P1sW0EPJcslw7UzRsiXL64w-O50Ed-RBICtay1g24M, or against GXWy8XF3vIml3_MfnmSmyuKBpT3B0dWbHRR_4cgq-gA when Build.TAGS contains dev-keys/test-keys. d3.d.l() calls it before requesting an integrity token. A one-line hook returns true unconditionally.

Impact

Lets an attacker run the Play Integrity attestation path from a repackaged build, or on a device without a genuine Play Store, so the backend receives an apparently valid token.

Evidence

d8/f.java; consumed at d3.d.java:527 if ((v7_2.enabled) && (d8.f.a(v3_6.signatures)))

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Do not gate attestation on a client-side certificate comparison. Request the token and let the server validate it, including appRecognitionVerdict.

INTE-03
High Anti-analysis

/proc/self/maps scan for Frida, Xposed, Riru, Zygisk and Substrate

How it works

The native code scans /proc/self/maps for a keyword set that is itself XOR-0x5A obfuscated: /proc/self/maps, xposed, lsposed, edxposed, riru, substrate, libcso_substrate, libbridge.so, zygisk; plus base64-encoded frida, gum-js-loop, libfrida-gadget, re.frida.server. It also looks for (deleted) libart/libc mappings and rwxp regions.

Impact

Detection, not prevention. Filtering open()/read()/fgets() and neutering strstr()/strcmp() makes every keyword invisible while the instrumented process runs normally.

Evidence

out/19_xor_decoded.txt (key 0x5A); referenced from x0018d3f7

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Instrumentation detection is an arms race with a known outcome. Spend the effort on server-side verification instead.

INTE-08
High Integrity

Play Integrity gate keyed on client-writable SharedPreferences

How it works

d3.d.l() only performs attestation when SP.getBoolean("SND",false) == true and SP.getInt("RID",0) == 3850153. Both live in the TOPFVC_Shared preferences file and are trivially hooked or edited on a rooted device.

Impact

The integrity requirement can be switched off entirely (skip attestation) or switched on with forged values, so the backend cannot distinguish a genuinely attested client from one that simply set two integers.

Evidence

d3.d.java:494 if ((!v4_3.a.getBoolean("SND",0)) || (v4_3.a.getInt("RID",0) != 3850153))

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Require a fresh, valid integrity token server-side on sensitive endpoints and reject requests without one — never let the client decide whether to attest.

STOR-03
High Data storage

Sensitive values in SharedPreferences with reversible obfuscation

How it works

TOPFVC_Shared (MODE_PRIVATE) holds ActiveID, ATFLogged, SingleTasking, Sign, RID, RD, SND, Aid, DeviceId, Pin, PinActive, Language, NewTaskType, ShowShop, AIT and RIT. Sign, Aid, DeviceId, RD and Pin are wrapped in d.q() — the keyless cipher of CRYP-01 — and unwrapped with d.p().

Impact

The backend base URL and pin, the integrity gate values (SND/RID), the cached attestation token (RD/RIT/AIT) and the device identity are all readable and writable by the app itself, and all included in backups.

Evidence

helper/a0.x0()..x4(), ha.h, d3.d.l(), com.bumptech.glide.manager.r

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Use EncryptedSharedPreferences with an AndroidKeyStore master key, and exclude the file from backup.

STOR-07
High Credentials

WebView used for Instagram login and hCaptcha with a JS bridge

How it works

WebViewActivity loads https://www.instagram.com/ for the cookie-harvest login path (CRED-03) and also renders hCaptcha from an embedded HTML template using jsSrc https://js.hcaptcha.com/1/api.js, exposing a JSInterface bridge object with getConfig(), onPass(token), onError(), onLoaded() and onOpen(). The template mocks the bridge when it is absent and reads window.JSDI.getDebugInfo().

Impact

A JavaScript bridge in a WebView that displays a live Instagram session is a credential-exposure channel: any injected or compromised script in that context can read cookies and call back into the app. The hCaptcha bridge hands the token straight to native code, so a token can be injected without solving a challenge.

Evidence

oa.l1 / com.nivaroid.topfollow.ui.WebViewActivity; embedded hCaptcha HTML template in the DEX string table containing BridgeObject = window.JSInterface

Proof of concept

04_credential_theft.js 02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js

Remediation

Do not run third-party login flows in an in-app WebView. Use Custom Tabs so the browser owns the session, restrict addJavascriptInterface to bound methods, and verify captcha tokens server-side only.

STOR-15
High Privacy

No user confirmation before credential-driven automation begins

How it works

Once an account row exists with active=1, DoTasksService.onCreate() collects it and a() starts a ja.e worker immediately; the only gate is the SingleTasking preference deciding whether one or all workers start. There is no per-action, per-session or per-account re-consent before the app begins following, liking, commenting and viewing stories as that user.

Impact

Users hand over a password or session and the app then acts autonomously and continuously on their behalf with no meaningful ongoing consent — a serious informed-consent and ToS exposure for the user.

Evidence

DoTasksService.onCreate()/a(); instagram_accounts.active; SharedPreferences "SingleTasking"

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Require explicit, per-session, revocable consent for each automated action category, and show the user a running audit log.

TRAN-01
High Transport

Certificate pinning defeated at the Java layer

How it works

Pinning is implemented as okhttp3.CertificatePinner instances constructed from native code via JNI. CertificatePinner.check() is an ordinary Java method, so replacing its implementation with a no-op accepts any certificate, and hooking CertificatePinner$Builder.add() reveals every pin as it is installed.

Impact

Full MITM of both the Instagram private API and the TopFollow backend, exposing every credential, token and coin claim in cleartext to an attacker on the same network.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so references certificatePinner / CertificatePinner$Builder.add; pin literal sha256/d845591e...; no TLS imports in the .so (CRYP-05)

Proof of concept

02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js

Remediation

Perform verification in native code against a pinned SPKI set, and validate the pin list itself with a build-time signature.

BIZ-09
Medium Business logic

VIP upgrade gated only on locally available fields

How it works

account/upgradeAccountToVip.php is called only if currentInstagram().getMedia_count() and getUsername() are non-empty, and the body carries vip_stamp = helper.q.m() (x0011f42b) — a native value with no visible per-account or per-payment binding. The local instagram_accounts.is_vip column mirrors the status.

Impact

The precondition is data the client already holds, and the stamp is opaque but constant per build; combined with a local is_vip flag the VIP tier is weakly protected at best.

Evidence

androidx.fragment.app.e.java:381 p8.addProperty("vip_stamp", q.m()) after a media_count/username emptiness test; instagram_accounts.is_vip

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Bind VIP status to a server-side payment record and re-validate it on every privileged call.

BIZ-10
Medium Business logic

Coupon, gift-code and daily-reward redemption driven by the client

How it works

account/addCoupon.php, getCoupons.php, getGiftCodeReward.php, checkDailyGift.php and getDailyItems.php are all invoked with client-built bodies; the coupon code arrives as a plain coupon/coupon_type property and the models Coupon, CouponList, CouponType are parsed client-side.

Impact

Codes can be enumerated and replayed, and daily-gift timing checks run against the device clock, which the user controls.

Evidence

androidx.fragment.app.e cases 3, 7, 9, 13, 15; addProperty("coupon_type", ...)

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Rate-limit and single-use every code server-side; take the daily-reset time from the server, not the device clock.

BIZ-11
Medium Business logic

Invite, leaderboard and miner-request flows are client-editable

How it works

account/getInviteData.php, setInviteCode.php, getLeaderBoard.php, getMinerRequests.php and changeMinerRequest.php all take a client-built JSON body with a locally generated request_id UUID (UUID.randomUUID()) and a by/user_pk identity field.

Impact

Referral rewards and rankings can be inflated by spoofing user_pk/by; a locally generated request_id provides no deduplication guarantee unless the server enforces it.

Proof of concept

Remediation

CRED-09
Medium Cryptography

Android Keystore ECDSA key under a hard-coded alias

How it works

An ECDSA P-256 key pair is generated in the Android Keystore under the fixed alias top_key_4286, and the app references Instagram's attestation/create_android_keystore/ endpoint.

Impact

A constant alias makes the key predictable and enumerable across installs; combined with the attestation endpoint it indicates an attempt to obtain Instagram device attestation that cannot actually be validated.

Evidence

Keystore alias literal top_key_4286; endpoint attestation/create_android_keystore/ in the DEX string table

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Derive the alias per install from a server-issued nonce and enable setAttestationChallenge so the key is hardware-attested.

CRED-11
Medium Credentials

Dead JNI bridge: native code looks up helper.T.digest([B)[B which does not exist in the DEX

How it works

At arm64 VAs 0x106660 and 0x162c4c (and the equivalents at 0x106714 and 0x162ce0) libtopfollow.so executes ldr x8,[x8,#0x108] — JNIEnv function-table slot 33, GetStaticMethodID — with x1 = the class ref for com/nivaroid/topfollow/helper/T, x2 = "digest" (.rodata+0x14b88) and x3 = "([B)[B" (.rodata+0x14cc4). Enumerating the DEX directly shows helper.T declares exactly two methods, o(Ljava/lang/String;)Ljava/lang/String; and sd(Ljava/lang/String;)Ljava/lang/String;. There is no digest method, and no method with descriptor ([B)[B exists in any com.nivaroid.topfollow class. The lookup therefore returns NULL and raises NoSuchMethodError.

Impact

The native layer expects a Java static byte[] digest(byte[]) that the shipped DEX does not provide. That is a build-integrity defect: native and Java halves of the app are out of step, so either a code path silently fails at runtime (and whatever protection or transform it was meant to perform does not happen), or the Java class was stripped by an obfuscator that renamed or removed a method the native side still references by string. Either way it demonstrates that the native/Java contract is not verified at build time, and it is exactly the kind of mismatch an attacker can exploit by supplying their own digest implementation in a repackaged build to control what the native code believes it hashed.

Evidence

Disassembly at 0x106644-0x106664 and 0x162c30-0x162c50: ldr x8,[x8,#0x108] then add x2,x2,#0xb88 (digest), add x3,x3,#0xcc4 (([B)[B), blr x8; DEX enumeration of Lcom/nivaroid/topfollow/helper/T; returning only o and sd; zero ([B)[B methods in the app's packages

Proof of concept

10_java_crypto_layer.js 07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Add a build-time check that every FindClass/Get*MethodID target in the native code exists in the compiled DEX, and fail the build otherwise. Exclude natively-referenced classes from obfuscator renaming.

CRYP-06
Medium Obfuscation

Base64-only obfuscation of endpoint paths and constants

How it works

Sensitive endpoint names are stored as single-layer base64 decoded by d.o(): b3JkZXIvc3VibWl0T3JkZXIucGhw=order/submitOrder.php, aW5zdGFncmFtTG9naW4ucGhw=instagramLogin.php, Z2V0TWFpbkluZm8ucGhw=getMainInfo.php, YWNjb3VudC9jaGVja0NhcHRjaGEucGhw=account/checkCaptcha.php, cHJlLWxvZ2luL3NldFVwRGV2aWNlLnBocA===pre-login/setUpDevice.php, cHJlLWxvZ2luL2FjdGl2ZURldmljZS5waHA==pre-login/activeDevice.php, cHJlLWxvZ2luL3ByaXZhY3lQb2xpY3kucGhw=pre-login/privacyPolicy.php. Some are nested two or three deep.

Impact

Provides zero protection — the entire API surface was recovered with a shell one-liner — while actively hindering legitimate security review and audit of the app.

Evidence

com.bumptech.glide.d.o(String) call sites in ha.a, ha.b, a7.a, androidx.fragment.app.e

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Obfuscating the API surface is not a control. Ship a documented, authenticated API instead.

CRYP-07
Medium Native hardening

Native string tables obfuscated with two single-byte XOR keys

How it works

An exhaustive sweep of every printable run in libtopfollow.so against all 127 single-byte XOR keys recovers the whole secret set with just two keys: 0x55 yields the backend URL https://top.nivafollower.app/v840/, the Instagram URLs, a UUID 3bbeeba8e-beaa-4458-ac60-6d9a61b2be9e and the pin; 0x5A yields the anti-Frida keyword list and every root-detection path.

Impact

Single-byte XOR is defeated by a brute force that runs in well under a second, so all “hidden” native strings are effectively plaintext. The protection only stops the most casual strings(1) run.

Evidence

out/19_xor_decoded.txt (sweep of all ASCII runs, keys 1..127); per-function reference map out/18_native_strings_by_fn.txt (943 PIC thunks, 760 resolved .rodata references)

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

If strings must be hidden, derive them at runtime from a keyed KDF with per-install entropy, or fetch them inside an attested server session.

CRYP-11
Medium Cryptography

Instagram password sealed with RSA PKCS#1 v1.5 rather than OAEP

How it works

q8.t1.f(String plaintext, String pubKeyB64, String keyId) is the app's Instagram password encryptor and produces the #PWD_INSTAGRAM:4:<ts>:<base64> envelope. It generates a 32-byte AES key and a 12-byte IV from SecureRandom, wraps the AES key with Cipher.getInstance("RSA/ECB/PKCS1PADDING"), then encrypts the password with Cipher.getInstance("AES/GCM/NoPadding") using GCMParameterSpec(128, iv) and updateAAD(ts.getBytes()). The blob is 0x01 || keyId || iv[12] || u16le(encKeyLen) || encKey || tag[16] || ciphertext. The construction is sound — authenticated encryption with the timestamp bound as additional authenticated data — but the key-wrapping layer uses PKCS#1 v1.5 padding instead of RSA-OAEP.

Impact

PKCS#1 v1.5 key transport is the padding variant with a long history of padding-oracle and Bleichenbacher-style attacks; OAEP is the recommended replacement. In this specific deployment the practical exposure is limited because the ciphertext is consumed only by Instagram's own servers and the app never exposes a decryption oracle, but it means the strongest layer of the password's protection rests on a deprecated padding scheme. This is inherited from Instagram's protocol rather than chosen by the vendor, which is itself the point: the vendor has reimplemented a confidential protocol and inherits its weaknesses without owning them.

Evidence

q8.t1.java:56 Cipher.getInstance("RSA/ECB/PKCS1PADDING"); q8.t1.java:59-61 AES/GCM/NoPadding + GCMParameterSpec(128, iv) + updateAAD; called from ia.v.java:259 params.put("password", q8.t1.f(...))

Proof of concept

10_java_crypto_layer.js

Remediation

Use Instagram's official OAuth instead of reimplementing its private password-sealing protocol. If the protocol must be implemented, prefer OAEP where the server allows it and pin the expected key length.

INTE-04
Medium Anti-analysis

Root detection is a static list of nine su paths

How it works

The check tests for Superuser.apk, /sbin/su, /system/bin/su, /system/xbin/su, /data/local/xbin/su, /data/local/bin/su, /system/sd/xbin/su, /system/bin/failsafe/su and /data/local/su. The paths are XOR-0x5A encoded in .rodata and probed through open/access/stat and java.io.File.exists().

Impact

Magisk DenyList, a renamed su, or a KernelSU/APatch install is invisible to this list. Forcing the probes to fail with ENOENT defeats it completely.

Evidence

out/19_xor_decoded.txt (key 0x5A root-path block)

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Use the Play Integrity deviceRecognitionVerdict fields evaluated server-side; a static path list is trivially evaded.

INTE-05
Medium Anti-analysis

Emulator detection from spoofable Build properties

How it works

The native bootstrap reads Build.DEVICE and Build.HARDWARE (referenced as DEVICE/HARDWARE in the function's string set) to reject emulators.

Impact

android.os.Build fields are plain statics; overwriting them in Frida makes any emulator report itself as a Samsung SM-E625F on exynos9825 — which is exactly the identity the app already hard-codes for Instagram.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so!x0018d3f7 string references DEVICE, HARDWARE; matching hard-coded UA in ia.q

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Collect emulator signals server-side from attestation, not from client-readable system properties.

INTE-09
Medium Integrity

Integrity token cached for six hours and replayable

How it works

The attestation result is cached in SharedPreferences RIT/AIT/RD and reused while now - RIT < 6 h. The cached token is then re-sent as body field x2 = d.q(d.p(SP"RD")).

Impact

One valid token can be replayed for six hours across unlimited requests, so the attestation provides no per-request binding and no protection against a scripted client that captured a single genuine token.

Evidence

d3.d.java:500-538: getLong("RIT",0) freshness test, p21.addProperty("x2", d.q(d.p(SP"RD")))

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Bind each token to a server-issued nonce and a request hash, use it once, and expire it in minutes.

INTE-10
Medium Integrity

Predictable Play Integrity nonce

How it works

The integrity nonce is RD + helper.q.j() + "877665803231", where q.j() is a native timestamp and 877665803231 is a constant GCP project number. There is no server-supplied randomness.

Impact

A predictable nonce lets an attacker pre-compute or reuse attestation requests and prevents the server from establishing freshness — which is the whole point of a nonce.

Evidence

d3.d.l() nonce composition; constant 877665803231

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Issue a cryptographically random per-request nonce from the server and embed it in the integrity request.

Medium Privacy

Native bootstrap collects ANDROID_ID and a full device profile

How it works

x0018d3f7 resolves references to ANDROID_ID, DeviceModel and its setters setHash_key, setNonce, setHash_type, addDevice, populating the Room device table (coin, gem, hash_type, hash_key, nonce, token, fcm_token).

Impact

A persistent cross-session device fingerprint is built and uploaded, enabling tracking of users across installs and correlation of their Instagram accounts.

INTE-12
Medium Integrity

Self-signed 25-year signing certificate, serial 1, v2-only signature

How it works

The APK is signed with a self-signed RSA-2048 certificate whose subject and issuer are both CN=Maryam Ahmadi, OU=Android Developer, O=NivaRoid, L=Shiraz, ST=Fars, C=IR, serial number 1, valid 2023-12-15 → 2048-12-08, SHA-256 d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e. There is no v1/JAR signature at all (no META-INF/*.RSA|*.SF|*.MF) — only an APK Signature Scheme v2 block plus a verity padding block.

Impact

A 25-year self-signed key with serial 1 is generated once and never rotated; if it leaks, every past and future build can be forged and there is no revocation path. v2-only signing also means older tooling sees the APK as unsigned.

Evidence

APK Signing Block: magic at 0x9d1608, v2 pair at 0x9ce620, cert DER 864 bytes; parsed in out/20_signing.txt

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Use Play App Signing so Google holds the app signing key, keep the upload key in an HSM, and rotate on a defined schedule.

INTE-13
Medium Integrity

ECDSA device-attestation key uses a fixed alias and a 25-year self-signed certificate

How it works

com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.T performs the app's device attestation against the AndroidKeyStore under the hardcoded alias top_key_4286. T.o(String) loads the keystore, fetches getCertificate("top_key_4286") and getCertificateChain("top_key_4286"), signs the input with SHA256withECDSA using getKey("top_key_4286", null), and returns Base64( signature + "#" + Base64(pubkey.getEncoded()) + "#" + JSONArray(Base64(cert[i].getEncoded())) ). T.sd(String) returns just the signature. The native layer drives this through q.i(JsonObject,String) / x00120b1e, and d3.d.java:738-740 generates the EC key pair and reads ECPublicKey.getW(). The alias is a compile-time constant identical on every installation.

Impact

A fixed, guessable keystore alias means the attestation key is addressable by any code in the process, and the attestation is self-asserted: the app sends its own public key and its own certificate chain alongside the signature, so the server learns the key from the same untrusted channel it uses to verify it. Because the certificate chain is generated on-device rather than issued by a hardware attestation root, nothing binds the key to genuine StrongBox or TEE-backed storage — a repackaged build, an emulator or a rooted device can mint an identical-looking chain. The 25-year validity of the signing certificate elsewhere in this app (2023-12-15 to 2048-12-08) shows the project's posture toward key lifetimes.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.T.o()/sd(); alias literal top_key_4286; d3.d.java:738-740 KeyPairGenerator.getInstance("EC") + ECPublicKey.getW(); native driver q.i = x00120b1e

Proof of concept

10_java_crypto_layer.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Use hardware-backed key attestation: generate the key with setIsStrongBoxBacked(true) and an attestation challenge issued by the server, then verify the certificate chain on the server up to the Google hardware attestation root and check attestationSecurityLevel. Derive the alias per-installation.

INTE-14
Medium Integrity

Native anti-analysis relies on file reads and string matching; no ptrace, TracerPid, property_get, dlopen or timing checks exist

How it works

The claimed detection set does not match the binary. Across all three ABIs the string tables contain no ptrace, no TracerPid, no /proc/self/status, no property_get or __system_property_get, no dlopen/dlsym, no prctl, no clock_gettime or gettimeofday. The 90-symbol import list confirms it: the only timing-adjacent import is clock, and the only syscall-adjacent ones are syscall, __open_2, read, __read_chk, close and access. What the library actually does is read /proc/self/maps and match keywords that are stored XOR-obfuscated with key 0x5A (recovered in work/out/19_xor_decoded.txt), match base64-encoded Frida markers — decoding the .rodata base64 blobs yields libfrida-gadget, re.frida.server, gum-js-loop, substrate, libcso_substrate, libbridge.so, libc.so (deleted) and libart.so (deleted) — and access() nine hardcoded su paths. There is no inline-hook detection, no SVC-based syscall path, no debugger-attached check and no execution-timing check.

Impact

Every detection in the native layer is a filesystem read followed by a substring match, which is the weakest possible construction: it is defeated by sanitising the buffer returned from read(), by hooking strstr/strcmp, or by simply not having the target strings present. Because there is no ptrace self-attach, no TracerPid parse and no timing check, a tracer can attach and single-step without ever tripping a detection. The obfuscation budget went entirely into control-flow flattening of code that performs trivially bypassable checks, so the anti-analysis story is far weaker than the binary's apparent sophistication suggests.

Evidence

String and import sweeps over all three ABIs; DT_NEEDED = libz, libandroid, liblog, libm, libdl, libc; 90 imported symbols enumerated in work/out/30_aes_forensics_arm64.txt section 8; base64-decoded anti-analysis markers; XOR key 0x5A keyword table in work/out/19_xor_decoded.txt

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js 07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Treat detection as telemetry for server-side risk scoring rather than as a gate. If native detection is retained, add checks that cannot be satisfied by sanitising a file read — hardware attestation verified server-side is the only construction that holds.

STOR-05
Medium Server-side

get_image.php accepts a client-supplied image_url

How it works

ha.i POSTs to get_image.php with a body containing image_url (six addProperty("image_url", ...) sites), request_id, user_pk and by. The server is expected to fetch that URL and return an image.

Impact

A server-side request forgery primitive: an attacker can point image_url at internal addresses, cloud metadata endpoints (169.254.169.254) or arbitrary third parties, using the vendor's server as a proxy.

Evidence

ha.i.java:44-45; addProperty("image_url", ...) call sites

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Validate and allow-list the URL server-side, resolve and block private/link-local ranges, disable redirects, and cap response size and content type.

STOR-08
Medium Business logic

CAPTCHA site keys come from native code and the token is signed client-side

How it works

CaptchaRequest chooses between reCAPTCHA (captcha_type == 0) and hCaptcha, obtaining the site key from native helper.q.f() (x0010e27f) and helper.q.g() (x00113f7a). On success the token goes to verifyCaptcha(), which signs it with native helper.q.c(token) (x00105e9b) and sends captcha_stamp to account/checkCaptcha.php. The SDK fallback site key 10000000-ffff-ffff-ffff-000000000001 is present in the embedded template.

Impact

The token can be supplied directly to verifyCaptcha() by a hook, bypassing the challenge UI entirely. Whether that succeeds depends solely on checkCaptcha.php validating the token with the provider — unverifiable from the client — and the native signing step adds no provider binding.

Evidence

com.nivaroid.topfollow.views.CaptchaRequest (showReCaptcha, showHCaptcha, verifyCaptcha); ha.a.java:484 addProperty("captcha_stamp", q.c(token))

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Validate the captcha token server-side against the provider's siteverify API with the secret key, bound to the session and to the action being performed.

STOR-09
Medium Integrity

Task-control service accepts an arbitrary account id with no authorisation check

How it works

DoTasksService.onStartCommand() reads action (stop/enable/disable) and, for enable/disable, an integer extra id that it passes to MyDatabase.setup().p(id) to fetch an InstagramAccount and start automation for it. The service also broadcasts "task.service.receiver" with type=start|stop and setPackage("com.nivaroid.topfollow"). Neither the service nor TaskActionReceiver is exported, so this is an internal surface — but the design accepts an arbitrary account id with no authorisation binding.

Impact

Any component inside the app — including one reached through a WebView bridge or a hijacked intent relay — can start or stop credential-driven automation for any stored account by index.

Evidence

DoTasksService.onStartCommand(): getExtras().getString("action"), getExtras().getInt("id") → new ja.e(MyDatabase.setup().p(id), ...); broadcast "task.service.receiver"

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js

Remediation

Validate that the caller is authorised for the requested account, keep the receiver unexported, and use in-process messaging instead of a broadcast.

STOR-10
Medium Business logic

No client-side throttling on the money endpoints

How it works

Claims (order/syncOrder.php), orders (order/submitOrder.php), coupons and daily gifts are issued back-to-back from worker threads and UI handlers with no client-side throttle beyond app_info.action_delay — a server-supplied integer used only to pace Instagram actions. request_id is a locally generated UUID.

Impact

A scripted client can submit claims at arbitrary rate. Exploitability depends entirely on server-side controls of which the client shows no evidence, and the locally generated request_id offers no deduplication unless enforced.

Evidence

ha.c, ha.b, ha.h; app_info.action_delay column; UUID.randomUUID() request ids

Proof of concept

05_coin_economy_bypass.js 06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Enforce per-account and per-device rate limits, server-issued idempotency keys, and anomaly detection on claim velocity.

STOR-12
Medium Instagram API

Login response parsed by substring matching on the raw JSON text

How it works

ia.s.success() decides the login outcome by string-searching the raw response body for "Bearer", "two_step_verification", "challenge_required", "The password you entered is incorrect", "We can't find an account with" and "there was a problem with your request", and extracts the 2FA context with indexOf("(dkc") plus the regex (\d+)\s+"com\.bloks\.. ia.v.a() likewise splits on "logged_in_user", "headers", "Bearer" and "hmac".

Impact

Fragile parsing of attacker-influenceable text. Any MITM (TRAN-01) or crafted Instagram response can steer the client into the two-step-verification branch, inject a chosen challenge_context, instance_id or marker_id, or cause a bearer token to be extracted from an arbitrary position in the body.

Evidence

ia.s.success(InstagramBody); ia.v.a(ia.v,String); constants instance_id 4806706160933748992, marker_id 36707139

Proof of concept

03_instagram_api_intercept.js 06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Parse responses with a real JSON parser, validate against a schema, and reject unexpected shapes instead of substring-matching.

STOR-14
Medium Instagram API

Direct-message, Threads and interop endpoints automated

How it works

Beyond follow/like/comment/save/seen/repost the app references direct_v2/has_interop_upgraded/, create_note/v2/ (Threads notes), seen/, /save/, three Facebook doc_id GraphQL documents and the token prefix 3,IGbdd5f76a8fb9c4f86adf36a09f2750dc,{pk}, and stores interop_messaging_user_fbid and fbid_v2 per account.

The automation reaches into Instagram DM interop and Threads, widening the abuse surface well beyond follower exchange and exposing Facebook-side identifiers that link the Instagram and Facebook accounts.

DEX string table: direct_v2/has_interop_upgraded/, create_note/v2/, seen/, /save/, 3,IGbdd5f76a8fb9c4f86adf36a09f2750dc,{pk}; instagram_accounts.interop_messaging_user_fbid, fbid_v2

TRAN-03
Medium Transport

No networkSecurityConfig declared

How it works

The manifest sets android:usesCleartextTraffic="false" but declares no android:networkSecurityConfig. There is therefore no certificate-transparency requirement, no explicit trust-anchor restriction and no per-domain policy; on API 24+ user-installed CAs are excluded by default, but the app's own WebView and any debug-build behaviour is unconstrained.

Impact

The only transport control is the runtime-installed OkHttp pinner, which is itself hookable (TRAN-01) and remotely replaceable (TRAN-02). A declarative policy would at least survive Java-layer hooking.

Evidence

out/07_AndroidManifest.xml: usesCleartextTraffic="false", networkSecurityConfig absent

Proof of concept

02_ssl_pinning_bypass.js

Remediation

Add a network_security_config.xml pinning the backend and Instagram domains, with <pin-set> entries, a backup pin and an expiration date.

TRAN-04
Medium Transport

Response host/path verification is a native function over a client-held object

How it works

helper.q.e() (x0014e2e9) is referenced alongside getHost, getPath and web, indicating a post-response check that the reply came from the expected host and path. It runs on the client, over a response object the client already holds.

Impact

A client-side check of a value the client controls is not a control: with Frida attached it returns whatever the caller wants, so it provides no assurance that traffic was not redirected.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so!x0014e2e9 string references getHost, getPath, web; Java wrapper helper.q.e()

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Verify origin server-side, or rely on a declarative network security config plus native TLS verification.

TRAN-05
Medium Transport

Instagram private-API traffic multiplexed with backend traffic in one process

How it works

Three Retrofit instances for Instagram (b.i.instagram.com/api/v1/, i.instagram.com/api/v2/, www.instagram.com/graphql/query) and one for the vendor backend share a single OkHttp stack built by the same native bootstrap; d.t() headers go to the backend while ia.q.f() headers go to Instagram.

Impact

One successful MITM or one hooked interceptor yields both the Instagram session material and the TopFollow account material simultaneously; there is no isolation between the two trust domains.

Evidence

helper.q.l(0|1|2) → x0018d3f7; helper.q.k() → x00126f7c; ia.q.f() vs d.t()

Proof of concept

03_instagram_api_intercept.js 08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Separate the trust domains: never let the vendor backend see Instagram session material (CRED-04), and use distinct OkHttp clients with distinct pin sets.

CRED-10
Low Data storage

Firebase cloud project number and FCM token collected

How it works

The Play Integrity nonce is composed as RD + q.j() + "877665803231", where 877665803231 is the Google Cloud project number, and device.fcm_token is stored and uploaded.

Impact

Leaks the vendor's GCP project (abusable for quota/abuse reports and for correlating installs) and gives the operator a push channel to every device.

Evidence

d3.d.l(): literal 877665803231; Room DDL device(... `fcm_token` TEXT)

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Do not embed the project number in a client-side nonce; use a server-issued random nonce per request.

CRYP-08
Low Native hardening

Instagram URLs hidden as triple-base64 in .rodata

How it works

https://b.i.instagram.com/api/v1/, https://i.instagram.com/api/v2/, https://www.instagram.com/graphql/query, https://www.instagram.com/ and the endpoint fragments create_note/v2/, seen/, /save/ are each base64-encoded three times before being embedded.

Impact

Layered base64 is not encryption; it decoded deterministically. It documents the full private-API surface the app abuses.

Evidence

libtopfollow.so .rodata; decoded via out/19_xor_decoded.txt and the bootstrap function x0018d3f7

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

See CRYP-07.

CRYP-13
Low Cryptography

Native AES is table-based with no ARMv8 Crypto Extension, so it is cache-timing exposed

How it works

A full instruction sweep of arm64 .text (397,892 instructions) found zero occurrences of aese, aesd, aesmc, aesimc, sha1* or sha256*. Combined with the absence of 32-bit T-tables (Te0[0]=0xc66363a5, Td0[0]=0x51f4a750 and their byte-swapped forms are all not present) this identifies the implementation as the compact 256-byte-S-box variant rather than a bitsliced or hardware-accelerated one. The DT_NEEDED list is libz, libandroid, liblog, libm, libdl, libc — no crypto library — and the 90 imported symbols contain no cryptographic function.

Impact

S-box lookups indexed by secret-dependent bytes are the classic AES cache-timing side channel. On a shared device another process in the same cache domain can recover the key from lookup timing. The practical severity is low because the cipher is internal to the native pipeline rather than protecting long-lived secrets at rest, and exploiting it needs a co-resident attacker — but it also means the app forgoes the ARMv8 AES instructions that every supported device (minSdk 24) provides, so it is slower and weaker than the platform default for no benefit.

Evidence

Capstone sweep of arm64 .text: no aese/aesd/aesmc/aesimc/sha* (work/out/30_aes_forensics_arm64.txt section 5); T-table constants absent (section 2); DT_NEEDED and 90-symbol import list contain no crypto (section 8)

Proof of concept

09_native_aes_dump.js

Remediation

Use the ARMv8 Crypto Extension (constant-time by construction) or BoringSSL via the platform. If a software table implementation must stay, use a bitsliced variant and mask the lookups.

CRYP-14
Low Cryptography

Opaque 5-byte literal 'F3AES' embedded in the JNI metadata cluster of all three ABIs and referenced by nothing

How it works

A 5-byte NUL-terminated string F3AES sits at arm64 .rodata+0x14b2f, x86_64 +0x0fdff and x86 +0x092ef. Its immediate neighbours are unmistakably JNI registration metadata: (Lretrofit2/Response;)Ljava/lang/String;, (ZLjava/lang/String;)Lretrofit2/Retrofit;, digest, com/nivaroid/topfollow/helper/T, setup, ()Lcom/nivaroid/topfollow/models/InstagramAccount;. An exhaustive ADRP+ADD/ADR/LDR resolution sweep of the entire arm64 .text found no instruction that computes the address of F3AES, and the string does not appear anywhere in the DEX. It also does not decode as base64 under any alignment or prefix.

Impact

The name reads as a cipher-suite or algorithm identifier, and it is placed among live JNI metadata, yet nothing in the binary or the DEX consumes it. That is either a dead remnant of a removed or disabled cryptographic feature — in which case the shipping binary carries unreferenced crypto naming that will mislead any audit, including this one, and may indicate code paths still reachable by a future or server-flagged build — or it is consumed through a computed offset the static sweep cannot model, in which case an analyst cannot rule out an undisclosed algorithm selector. Both readings mean the binary contains crypto-related metadata whose liveness cannot be established statically.

Evidence

F3AES present in all three ABIs at the offsets above; zero resolving instructions across 397,892 arm64 .text instructions; absent from the DEX and from work/out/03_all_strings.txt; adjacent JNI metadata strings listed

Proof of concept

09_native_aes_dump.js 07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Strip unreferenced literals at link time (-ffunction-sections + --gc-sections). Do not leave dead crypto identifiers in a shipped binary; they cannot be distinguished from live ones by an auditor.

INTE-06
Low Native hardening

OLLVM control-flow flattening as the sole native protection

How it works

All 22 registered native functions use an OLLVM-style state-dispatcher (control-flow flattening), which makes static CFG recovery impractical in a disassembler. It does not protect data: resolving the 943 position-independent call $+5; pop reg; add reg,K thunks per function recovered 760 .rodata references and thus every secret string.

Impact

Raises analysis cost but not the security floor. Once the data references were resolved, the flattened control flow was irrelevant to extracting keys, URLs, pins and detection keywords.

Evidence

out/15_disasm_key.txt; thunk resolution work/fn_strings3.py → out/18_text_refs.json, out/18_native_strings_by_fn.txt

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Treat obfuscation as delay, never as a control. Combine it with hardware-backed attestation and server-side enforcement.

INTE-07
Low Anti-analysis

Scrambled DEX map_list to break static analysis tooling

How it works

The single classes.dex has a deliberately scrambled map_list, which makes annotation and section parsing crash or return out-of-bounds offsets in standard tooling.

Impact

An anti-RE measure with no security value: full pseudocode for all 4,146 classes was still recovered, and the scrambling merely removes the ability to read Retrofit annotations.

Evidence

Observed while parsing the DEX map list; annotation extraction goes out of bounds

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

Do not rely on tooling breakage. It inconveniences defenders and auditors more than attackers.

OBFU-02
Low Obfuscation

OLLVM control-flow flattening only: .text entropy is 6.86, so the library is not packed

How it works

Per-section entropy across arm64 libtopfollow.so shows .text = 6.8621 over 1,591,568 bytes and whole-file entropy = 6.7518. A packed or compressed .text scores above 7.8; 6.86 is the normal range for AArch64 code. The obfuscation is therefore purely control-flow: the AES wrapper at 0x34424 contains 1,085 instructions with a histogram of mov 191, movk 142, cmp 119, cset 46, b.eq 28, b.ne 23 and a single dispatch loop — the signature of OLLVM bogus-control-flow plus flattening. .rodata is only 37,547 bytes, so the claimed multi-megabyte white-box table cannot exist at any offset in a 1,805,400-byte file. The highest-entropy 4 KiB windows in the whole binary are 0x12000-0x13000 at 7.9954 and 0x13000-0x14000 at 7.9613 — and those two windows are precisely the AES S-box and inverse S-box, not a white-box table. .eh_frame yields 1,090 well-formed FDEs, which a packer would destroy.

Impact

The binary looks formidable — 1.8 MB, a single exported symbol, 22 runtime-registered natives, flattened control flow — but none of that is packing, and the absence of packing is what made the whole analysis possible statically. Every secret was recoverable without executing the app: 943 position-independent thunks resolved to 760 .rodata references, and a single-byte XOR sweep over keys 1-127 recovered the backend URL, the Instagram endpoints, the certificate pin and the anti-analysis keyword tables. Assessing this app's real strength requires separating obfuscation from protection: the former is heavy, the latter is thin.

Evidence

work/out/30_aes_forensics_arm64.txt sections 6-7 (per-section entropy, top entropy windows); instruction histogram of 0x34424; .rodata = 37,547 B; 1,090 .eh_frame FDEs from work/native_deep.py; no white-box table at the claimed offsets

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js 09_native_aes_dump.js

Remediation

Obfuscation is not a control. Spend the budget on server-side verification and hardware-backed attestation; client-side flattening only raises analysis cost, it does not change what an attacker who finishes the analysis can do.

STOR-04
Low Data storage

extractNativeLibs=false leaves the packed .so directly accessible

How it works

The manifest sets android:extractNativeLibs="false", so libtopfollow.so (arm64-v8a, x86_64, x86) is loaded straight out of the APK zip and is never unpacked into a protected directory.

Impact

The protection surface — all obfuscated strings, the pin, the detection keywords and the 22 JNI functions — can be lifted from the APK with unzip and analysed offline, which is exactly how this report was produced.

Evidence

out/07_AndroidManifest.xml; lib/{arm64-v8a,x86_64,x86}/libtopfollow.so and libdatastore_shared_counter.so

Proof of concept

07_native_jni_dumper.js

Remediation

This flag is a performance choice, not a security one. Do not treat native code presence as protection (see INTE-06).

STOR-06
Low Privacy

Crash reporting and analytics collect device and session data pre-consent

How it works

Firebase Crashlytics, Firebase Messaging, Firebase Sessions and androidx.datatransport (CCT backend) are all registered via androidx.startup initialisation providers and run before any user-consent gate.

Impact

Device identifiers, session data and crash traces — potentially including fragments of harvested credentials inside exception messages — leave the device automatically.

Evidence

Manifest meta-data com.google.firebase.components:*Registrar entries; authorities com.nivaroid.topfollow.firebaseinitprovider, com.nivaroid.topfollow.androidx-startup; backend:...CctBackendFactory = cct

Proof of concept

08_backend_traffic_and_servercheck.js

Remediation

Gate Crashlytics collection behind an explicit consent flag and scrub credential material from logs and exception messages.

STOR-11
Low Data storage

Balances and activity exposed through notification text and reflected errors

How it works

DoTasksService.b() renders a foreground notification containing live counters — "💰 Coins: " + DoTasksService.s + "\n✅ Tasks done: " + DoTasksService.t — from static mutable fields, and the app surfaces raw server error strings ("Sorry, there was a problem with your request.", "The password you entered is incorrect", "We can't find an account with") directly in the UI.

Impact

Account activity and balances are visible on the lock screen, and reflected server error text can leak account existence and state to anyone holding the device.

Evidence

DoTasksService.b(); static fields DoTasksService.s/.t; ia.s.success() error-string branches

Proof of concept

06_task_verification_bypass.js

Remediation

Mark the notification sensitive (visibility = VISIBILITY_PRIVATE) and map server errors to generic client-side messages.

STOR-13
Low Instagram API

Bloks/CAA login flow with hard-coded instance and marker ids

How it works

The login POST targets com.bloks.www.caa.login.login_homepage with bloks_version 083f38c334f42c5e3322bb77464c601e8882cd9ff2d30ac915ba7a497539d604, styles_id instagram and a server_params block containing INTERNAL__latency_qpl_instance_id 19558568600088, INTERNAL__latency_qpl_marker_id 36707139, offline_experiment_group caa_iteration_v3_perf_ig_4, access_flow_version pre_mt_behavior, login_surface login_home, login_entry_point logged_out, plus an aac sub-object (aac_init_timestamp, aacjid, aaccs).

Impact

Confirms the app is a full reimplementation of Instagram's confidential Android login flow rather than an OAuth client. Every constant is a fingerprint Instagram can use to detect and ban these sessions, and the hard-coded experiment ids will silently break as Instagram iterates.

Evidence

ia.v constructor and ia.s.success(); header x-ig-client-endpoint: com.bloks.www.caa.login.login_homepage

Proof of concept

03_instagram_api_intercept.js

Remediation

Use Instagram's official OAuth. Reimplementing the private login flow violates platform terms and puts the user's account at risk.

STOR-16
Low Attack surface

Small exported surface, but the launcher activity is world-reachable

How it works

The manifest exports exactly three components: com.nivaroid.topfollow.ui.TopActivity (MAIN/LAUNCHER, no permission), com.google.firebase.iid.FirebaseInstanceIdReceiver (protected by com.google.android.c2dm.permission.SEND) and androidx.profileinstaller.ProfileInstallReceiver (protected by android.permission.DUMP). There are no deep links, no content providers beyond the Firebase/androidx-startup initialisers, and no exported services; TaskActionReceiver and DoTasksService are not exported.

Impact

The direct IPC attack surface is small — a genuinely positive finding — but the two Firebase/androidx receivers are framework components whose protections are only as good as the platform's, and the launcher activity is the world-reachable entry point to the whole credential flow.

Evidence

out/07_AndroidManifest.xml; uses-permission set is INTERNET, FOREGROUND_SERVICE(_SPECIAL_USE), POST_NOTIFICATIONS, ACCESS_NETWORK_STATE, WAKE_LOCK, c2dm.RECEIVE plus a self-declared signature-level DYNAMIC_RECEIVER_NOT_EXPORTED_PERMISSION

Proof of concept

01_anti_tamper_killer.js

Remediation

Keep the receiver and service unexported, and validate every intent extra on the launcher path since it is world-reachable.

17. Dynamic analysis lab — 11 Frida scripts

Every script is self-contained and heavily commented with the exact classes, offsets and literals it targets. Load 00_common.js first; 01 and 02 are prerequisites for almost everything else because the app's bootstrap runs its checks inside JNI_OnLoad. Spawn mode is required — attaching after start misses the signature check and the Retrofit construction.

00_common.js — 123 lines

Shared helpers: Java class resolver, overload tracer, RegisterNatives capture, SharedPreferences dumper.

Show source
/* ============================================================================
 * TopFollow (com.nivaroid.topfollow v8.4.5-Beta / versionCode 845)
 * Dynamic-analysis lab - shared helpers
 * ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
 * Target facts used by every script (all recovered by static RE):
 *   package          : com.nivaroid.topfollow
 *   native lib       : libtopfollow.so  (JNI_OnLoad -> RegisterNatives x22,
 *                      all methods of com.nivaroid.topfollow.helper.q)
 *   backend base URL : https://top.nivafollower.app/v840/   (server-rotatable
 *                      via SharedPreferences "Pin"/"PinActive" + ServerCheckModel)
 *   IG private API   : https://b.i.instagram.com/api/v1/ , https://i.instagram.com/api/v2/
 *   IG graphql/web   : https://www.instagram.com/graphql/query , https://www.instagram.com/
 *   self-cert SHA-256: d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e
 *                      (== SHA-256 of the APK signing cert, CN=Maryam Ahmadi/O=NivaRoid)
 *   Room DB          : "t_f_d_b_f_v_c" -> tables device, instagram_accounts, two_factors, app_info
 *   SharedPrefs      : "TOPFVC_Shared"
 * ==========================================================================*/
'use strict';

const PKG   = 'com.nivaroid.topfollow';
const SONAME = 'libtopfollow.so';

const H = {
  log(tag, msg)  { console.log('[' + tag + '] ' + msg); },
  hr(t)          { console.log('\n=== ' + t + ' ' + '='.repeat(Math.max(0, 60 - t.length))); },

  /* Wait for a Java class that may only exist after the app's own bootstrap. */
  cls(name, cb) {
    Java.perform(function () {
      try { cb(Java.use(name)); }
      catch (e) {
        Java.enumerateLoadedClasses({
          onMatch: function (c) { if (c === name) { try { cb(Java.use(name)); } catch (_) {} } },
          onComplete: function () {}
        });
      }
    });
  },

  /* Hook every overload of a Java method and log args + return. */
  traceAll(klass, method, argNames) {
    Java.perform(function () {
      const K = Java.use(klass);
      const overloads = K[method].overloads;
      overloads.forEach(function (ov, idx) {
        ov.implementation = function () {
          const args = Array.prototype.slice.call(arguments);
          let s = klass + '.' + method + '#' + idx + '(';
          s += args.map(function (a, i) {
                 const n = (argNames && argNames[i]) ? argNames[i] : ('a' + i);
                 let v; try { v = (a === null || a === undefined) ? String(a) : a.toString(); }
                          catch (_) { v = '<' + a + '>'; }
                 if (v.length > 400) v = v.slice(0, 400) + '...(' + v.length + ' chars)';
                 return n + '=' + v;
               }).join(', ');
          s += ')';
          const r = ov.apply(this, arguments);
          let rs; try { rs = (r === null || r === undefined) ? String(r) : r.toString(); }
                  catch (_) { rs = '<obj>'; }
          if (rs.length > 600) rs = rs.slice(0, 600) + '...(' + rs.length + ' chars)';
          console.log('  >> ' + s + '\n  << ret=' + rs);
          return r;
        };
      });
      H.log('hook', klass + '.' + method + ' (' + overloads.length + ' overload(s))');
    });
  },

  /* Resolve the runtime address of a native helper.q JNI function.
   * helper.q.xNNNNNNNN are registered via RegisterNatives in JNI_OnLoad, so the
   * symbol is NOT in .dynsym - we intercept RegisterNatives to build the table. */
  natives: {},
  captureRegisterNatives(onReady) {
    const art = Process.findModuleByName('libart.so');
    if (!art) { H.log('!', 'libart.so not loaded yet'); return; }
    const cands = art.enumerateSymbols().filter(function (s) {
      return s.name.indexOf('RegisterNatives') !== -1 && s.name.indexOf('CheckJNI') === -1;
    });
    if (!cands.length) { H.log('!', 'RegisterNatives symbol not found'); return; }
    Interceptor.attach(cands[0].address, {
      onEnter: function (a) {
        const clazz = a[0], methods = a[2], n = a[3].toInt32();
        let cname = '<unknown>';
        try {
          const env = Java.vm.getEnv();
          cname = env.getClassName(clazz);
        } catch (_) {}
        for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
          const base = methods.add(i * Process.pointerSize * 3);
          const nameP = base.readPointer(), sigP = base.add(Process.pointerSize).readPointer(),
                fnP = base.add(Process.pointerSize * 2).readPointer();
          const nm = nameP.readCString(), sig = sigP.readCString();
          const mod = Process.findModuleByAddress(fnP);
          const off = mod ? fnP.sub(mod.base) : ptr(0);
          if (cname.indexOf('helper.q') !== -1 || cname.indexOf('topfollow') !== -1) {
            H.natives[cname + '.' + nm] = { fn: fnP, module: mod ? mod.name : '?', offset: off, sig: sig };
            H.log('JNI', cname + '.' + nm + sig + '  ->  ' + (mod ? mod.name : '?') + '!0x' + off.toString(16));
          }
        }
      },
      onLeave: function () { if (onReady) { const f = onReady; onReady = null; f(H.natives); } }
    });
    H.log('hook', 'art::JNI::RegisterNatives intercepted - waiting for JNI_OnLoad');
  },

  /* SharedPreferences("TOPFVC_Shared") dumper. */
  dumpPrefs(ctx) {
    const sp = ctx.getSharedPreferences('TOPFVC_Shared', 0);
    const all = sp.getAll();
    const it = all.entrySet().iterator();
    const out = {};
    while (it.hasNext()) { const e = it.next(); out[e.getKey().toString()] = String(e.getValue()); }
    return out;
  },

  appContext() {
    const ActivityThread = Java.use('android.app.ActivityThread');
    return ActivityThread.currentApplication().getApplicationContext();
  }
};

if (typeof module !== 'undefined') module.exports = H;

01_anti_tamper_killer.js — 270 lines

Defeats the signature self-check, /proc/self/maps scan, anti-Frida markers, root-path probes, emulator checks, Play-Store cert gate and the SND/RID integrity gate.

Show source
/* ============================================================================
 * 01 - ANTI-TAMPER / ANTI-FRIDA / ANTI-ROOT / SIGNATURE-CHECK KILLER
 * ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
 * Defeats every protection recovered from libtopfollow.so + DEX:
 *
 *  NATIVE (bootstrap mega-fn x0018d3f7 == helper.q.l(int), and friends):
 *    A. /proc/self/maps scan for XOR-0x5A encoded keywords:
 *         /proc/self/maps | xposed | lsposed | edxposed | riru | substrate |
 *         libcso_substrate | libbridge.so | zygisk
 *    B. base64-encoded Frida markers: frida | gum-js-loop | libfrida-gadget |
 *         re.frida.server        (+ TCP 27042/27043 default-port probes)
 *    C. root binary existence checks (XOR-0x5A):
 *         Superuser.apk /sbin/su /system/bin/su /system/xbin/su
 *         /data/local/xbin/su /data/local/bin/su /system/sd/xbin/su
 *         /system/bin/failsafe/su /data/local/su
 *    D. "(deleted)" libart/libc mappings + "rwxp" region checks (injected-code tell)
 *    E. APK signature verification:
 *         PackageManager.getPackageInfo(pkg, GET_SIGNATURES) ->
 *         MessageDigest("SHA-256").digest(sig.toByteArray()) compared against
 *         d845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e
 *         (= SHA-256 of the real signing cert: CN=Maryam Ahmadi, O=NivaRoid, IR)
 *    F. emulator / device-prop checks: Build.DEVICE, Build.HARDWARE
 *
 *  JAVA:
 *    G. d8.f.a(Signature[])  - Google "PhoneskyVerificationUtils" gate that must
 *         pass BEFORE Play Integrity is requested (hardcoded Play cert pins).
 *    H. d3.d.l(..) integrity gate: SharedPreferences SND==true && RID==3850153
 *
 * Usage:  frida -U -f com.nivaroid.topfollow -l 00_common.js -l 01_anti_tamper_killer.js
 *         (spawn mode is REQUIRED: the checks run inside JNI_OnLoad/bootstrap)

'use strict';

const H = (typeof module !== 'undefined' && module.exports) ? module.exports : H;
const EXPECTED_CERT_SHA256 =
  'd845591e086033a9035fd6b66c3c3d73aa33af90794d6b986e64779eea6bec5e';

/* ---------- A/B/C/D : filesystem + maps + string-compare neutering -------- */
const MAPS_DENY = ['frida', 'gum-js', 'gadget', 'linjector', 're.frida',
                   'xposed', 'lsposed', 'edxposed', 'riru', 'zygisk',
                   'substrate', 'libbridge', '(deleted)', 'rwxp', 'magisk',
                   'libhook', 'substrate'];
const ROOT_PATHS = ['superuser.apk', '/sbin/su', '/system/bin/su', '/system/xbin/su',
                    '/data/local/xbin/su', '/data/local/bin/su', '/system/sd/xbin/su',
                    '/system/bin/failsafe/su', '/data/local/su', 'supolicy', 'daemonsu',
                    '/system/app/superuser.apk', 'com.noshufou.android.su',
                    'com.thirdparty.superuser', 'eu.chainfire.supersu', 'com.koushikdutta.superuser'];

let blockedOpens = {};
function suspicious(path) {
  if (!path) return false;
  const p = path.toLowerCase();
  if (p.indexOf('/proc/self/maps') !== -1 || p.indexOf('/proc/self/task') !== -1 ||
      p.indexOf('/proc/self/status') !== -1 || p.indexOf('/proc/net/tcp') !== -1) return true;
  for (const r of ROOT_PATHS) if (p.indexOf(r) !== -1) return true;
  return false;
}

['open', 'open64', 'fopen', 'fopen64', '__open_2', 'access', 'stat', 'lstat', 'stat64'].forEach(function (fn) {
  const p = Module.findExportByName(null, fn);
  if (!p) return;
  Interceptor.attach(p, {
    onEnter: function (a) {
      let path = null;
      try { path = a[0].isNull() ? null : a[0].readCString(); } catch (_) {}
      this.path = path; this.deny = suspicious(path);
      if (this.deny) blockedOpens[path] = (blockedOpens[path] || 0) + 1;
    },
    onLeave: function (r) {
      if (!this.deny) return;
      if (this.path && this.path.toLowerCase().indexOf('/proc/self/maps') !== -1) {
        // let it succeed: we sanitise the *contents* on read() instead (see below)
        return;
      }
      // make root-binary / su probes fail with ENOENT
      r.replace(ptr(-1));
      try { Memory.writeS32(Module.findExportByName('libc.so', '__errno') ?
            Module.findExportByName('libc.so', '__errno')().readPointer() : ptr(0), 2); } catch (_) {}
    }
  });
});
H.log('anti-root', 'open/fopen/access/stat hooked (' + Object.keys(blockedOpens).length + ' denials so far)');

/* Sanitise /proc/self/maps + /proc/net/tcp contents byte-stream on read() */
const mapsReads = {};
const readPtr = Module.findExportByName('libc.so', 'read');
const fgetsPtr = Module.findExportByName('libc.so', 'fgets');
function scrub(buf, n) {
  if (n <= 0) return n;
  let s;
  try { s = buf.readUtf8String(n); } catch (_) { try { s = buf.readCString(); } catch (__) { return n; } }
  if (!s) return n;
  const low = s.toLowerCase();
  let hit = false;
  for (const d of MAPS_DENY) if (low.indexOf(d) !== -1) { hit = true; break; }
  if (!hit) return n;
  const clean = s.split('\n').filter(function (line) {
    const l = line.toLowerCase();
    for (const d of MAPS_DENY) if (l.indexOf(d) !== -1) return false;
    return true;
  }).join('\n');
  const cb = Memory.allocUtf8String(clean);
  Memory.copy(buf, cb, Math.min(n, clean.length + 1));
  return clean.length;
}
Interceptor.attach(readPtr, {
  onEnter: function (a) { this.fd = a[0].toInt32(); this.buf = a[1]; },
  onLeave: function (r) { const n = r.toInt32(); if (n > 0) { const m = scrub(this.buf, n); if (m !== n) r.replace(m); } }
});
if (fgetsPtr) Interceptor.attach(fgetsPtr, {
  onEnter: function (a) { this.buf = a[0]; this.n = a[1].toInt32(); },
  onLeave: function (r) { if (!r.isNull()) { const m = scrub(this.buf, this.n); } }
});
H.log('maps', '/proc/self/maps + /proc/net/tcp line-scrubbing installed');

/* strstr/strcmp/strncmp/memmem must never match a detection keyword */
['strstr', 'strcmp', 'strncmp', 'strcasecmp', 'memmem'].forEach(function (fn) {
  const p = Module.findExportByName('libc.so', fn);
  if (!p) return;
  Interceptor.attach(p, {
    onEnter: function (a) {
      this.deny = false;
      try {
        const hay = a[0].isNull() ? '' : a[0].readCString().toLowerCase();
        const nee = a[1].isNull() ? '' : a[1].readCString().toLowerCase();
        for (const d of MAPS_DENY.concat(ROOT_PATHS)) {
          if ((nee && nee.indexOf(d) !== -1) || (fn !== 'strstr' && hay.indexOf(d) !== -1 && nee === d)) {
            this.deny = true; break;
          }
        }
      } catch (_) {}
    },
    onLeave: function (r) { if (this.deny) r.replace(ptr(0)); }
  });
});
H.log('strcmp', 'strstr/strcmp/strncmp/memmem keyword-filtered');

/* B : TCP probe to default Frida ports -> connection refused */
const connectPtr = Module.findExportByName('libc.so', 'connect');
if (connectPtr) Interceptor.attach(connectPtr, {
  onEnter: function (a) {
    this.deny = false;
    try {
      const family = a[1].readU16();
      if (family === 2) { const port = (a[1].add(2).readU8() << 8) | a[1].add(3).readU8();
        if (port === 27042 || port === 27043 || port === 27044) { this.deny = true; H.log('frida-port', 'blocked connect to :' + port); } }
    } catch (_) {}
  },
  onLeave: function (r) { if (this.deny) r.replace(ptr(-1)); }
});

/* F : emulator / device-prop checks */
Java.perform(function () {
  const Build = Java.use('android.os.Build');
  const fake = { DEVICE: 'hero2lte', HARDWARE: 'exynos9825', MODEL: 'SM-E625F',
                 BRAND: 'samsung', MANUFACTURER: 'samsung', PRODUCT: 'f62',

  });

  H.log('emulator', 'android.os.Build spoofed -> samsung SM-E625F (matches the hardcoded IG User-Agent)');

  // File.exists() for root paths

    const p = this.getAbsolutePath().toLowerCase();

    return this.exists();
  };
  // Runtime.exec("su" / "which su")
  const RT = Java.use('java.lang.Runtime');
